Since the values $f(\mathbf{x}_k)$ decrease, the left side is at least $K\big(f(\mathbf{x}_K) - f^*\big)$.
\end{proof}

With $\kappa = \frac{L}{\mu}$, the condition number, the second rate is $(1 - \frac{1}{\kappa})^k \leq e^{-k/\kappa}$: the error decreases geometrically (``linear convergence''), and about $\kappa\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$ steps reduce it by the factor $\varepsilon$, as for quadratic functions (Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-quadratic}). Without strong convexity, the guarantee is only $O(\frac{1}{k})$: to halve the error, we have to double the number of steps. Adding a small ridge penalty $\lambda\norm{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^2$ to a convex loss makes it $2\lambda$-strongly convex, which gives the linear rate of part 2 instead of the rate of part 1, at the price of a slightly different minimum.

\subsection{Nonsmooth convex functions and the proximal gradient method}\label{sec:prox}

The $\ell_1$ norm, the hinge loss and $\ReLU$ are convex, but not differentiable everywhere. For convex functions, the gradient has a substitute.

\begin{definition}[Subgradient]\label{def:subgradient}
    Let $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ be convex. A vector $\mathbf{g}$ is a subgradient of $f$ at $\mathbf{x}$ if
    \[f(\mathbf{y}) \geq f(\mathbf{x}) + \mathbf{g}^\top(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}) \qquad \text{for all } \mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^n.\]
    The set $\partial f(\mathbf{x})$ of all subgradients at $\mathbf{x}$ is the subdifferential of $f$ at $\mathbf{x}$.
\end{definition}

A subgradient defines an affine function that lies below $f$ and touches it at $\mathbf{x}$. By the definition, $\mathbf{x}^*$ is a global minimum of $f$ if and only if $\mathbf{0} \in \partial f(\mathbf{x}^*)$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:subgradients}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item If $f$ is convex and differentiable, then $\partial f(\mathbf{x}) = \{\nabla f(\mathbf{x})\}$ for every $\mathbf{x}$.
        \item For $f(x) = |x|$ on $\mathbb{R}$: $\partial f(x) = \{1\}$ for $x > 0$, $\{-1\}$ for $x < 0$, and $\partial f(0) = [-1, 1]$.
        \item If $f(\mathbf{x}) = f_1(x_1) + \cdots + f_n(x_n)$ with convex functions $f_i$ of one variable, then $\mathbf{g} \in \partial f(\mathbf{x})$ if and only if $g_i \in \partial f_i(x_i)$ for all $i$. In particular, $\partial\norm{\cdot}_1(\mathbf{x})$ consists of the vectors $\mathbf{g}$ with $g_i = \sign(x_i)$ if $x_i \neq 0$ and $g_i \in [-1, 1]$ if $x_i = 0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    (1) By Theorem~\ref{thm:convex-first-order}, $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) \in \partial f(\mathbf{x})$. If $\mathbf{g} \in \partial f(\mathbf{x})$, then for every $\mathbf{v}$ and $t > 0$, $\frac{f(\mathbf{x} + t\mathbf{v}) - f(\mathbf{x})}{t} \geq \mathbf{g}^\top\mathbf{v}$, and as $t \to 0$, $\nabla f(\mathbf{x})^\top\mathbf{v} \geq \mathbf{g}^\top\mathbf{v}$. With $-\mathbf{v}$ instead of $\mathbf{v}$, we get equality, so $\mathbf{g} = \nabla f(\mathbf{x})$.

    (2) At $0$, $|y| \geq gy$ for all $y$ if and only if $|g| \leq 1$. For $x > 0$, $g = 1$ is a subgradient, since $|y| \geq y = x + (y - x)$. Conversely, if $g$ is a subgradient at $x > 0$, then for $|t| < x$, $x + t = |x + t| \geq x + gt$, i.e., $t \geq gt$ for positive and negative $t$, so $g = 1$. The case $x < 0$ is symmetric.

    (3) If $g_i \in \partial f_i(x_i)$ for all $i$, add the inequalities $f_i(y_i) \geq f_i(x_i) + g_i(y_i - x_i)$. Conversely, if $\mathbf{g} \in \partial f(\mathbf{x})$, use the definition with a vector $\mathbf{y}$ that differs from $\mathbf{x}$ only in its $i$-th entry. The last statement follows with (2).
\end{proof}

Machine learning losses are often a sum of a smooth part and a simple nonsmooth part, such as the lasso
\[F(\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}}^2 + \lambda\norm{\mathbf{w}}_1,\]
whose $\ell_1$ penalty produces sparse weights, i.e., many weights that are exactly $0$. For such sums, the optimality condition $\mathbf{0} \in \partial F(\mathbf{x})$ splits into the two parts.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:composite-optimality}
    Let $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ be convex and differentiable, and let $h: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ be convex. Then $\mathbf{x}^*$ minimizes $f + h$ if and only if $-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) \in \partial h(\mathbf{x}^*)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    If $-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) \in \partial h(\mathbf{x}^*)$, add the inequalities $f(\mathbf{y}) \geq f(\mathbf{x}^*) + \nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)^\top(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}^*)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:convex-first-order}) and $h(\mathbf{y}) \geq h(\mathbf{x}^*) - \nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)^\top(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}^*)$. Conversely, let $\mathbf{x}^*$ be a minimum, let $\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^n$, and let $\mathbf{x}_t = \mathbf{x}^* + t(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}^*)$ for $0 < t \leq 1$. By convexity, $h(\mathbf{x}_t) \leq h(\mathbf{x}^*) + t\big(h(\mathbf{y}) - h(\mathbf{x}^*)\big)$, so
    \[0 \leq \frac{(f + h)(\mathbf{x}_t) - (f + h)(\mathbf{x}^*)}{t} \leq \frac{f(\mathbf{x}_t) - f(\mathbf{x}^*)}{t} + h(\mathbf{y}) - h(\mathbf{x}^*).\]
    As $t \to 0$, we get $0 \leq \nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)^\top(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}^*) + h(\mathbf{y}) - h(\mathbf{x}^*)$, i.e., $-\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) \in \partial h(\mathbf{x}^*)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Proximal operator]\label{def:prox}
    Let $h$ be convex and $\eta > 0$. The proximal operator of $\eta h$ maps $\mathbf{v}$ to the minimum
    \[\operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}(\mathbf{v}) = \argmin_{\mathbf{u}} \Big(h(\mathbf{u}) + \frac{1}{2\eta}\norm{\mathbf{u} - \mathbf{v}}^2\Big),\]
    whenever it exists; it is unique, since the function in brackets is strictly convex.
\end{definition}

By Theorem~\ref{thm:composite-optimality} with $f(\mathbf{u}) = \frac{1}{2\eta}\norm{\mathbf{u} - \mathbf{v}}^2$, whose gradient is $\frac{1}{\eta}(\mathbf{u} - \mathbf{v})$, a point $\mathbf{p}$ is $\operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}(\mathbf{v})$ if and only if $\frac{1}{\eta}(\mathbf{v} - \mathbf{p}) \in \partial h(\mathbf{p})$. For $h = 0$, the proximal operator is the identity; it moves $\mathbf{v}$ towards smaller values of $h$, but not too far from $\mathbf{v}$.

\begin{proposition}[Soft thresholding]\label{prop:soft-threshold}
    The proximal operator of $\eta\lambda\norm{\cdot}_1$ acts on each entry by soft thresholding:
    \[\operatorname{prox}_{\eta\lambda\norm{\cdot}_1}(\mathbf{v})_i = S_{\eta\lambda}(v_i), \qquad S_\tau(v) = \begin{cases} v - \tau & \text{if } v > \tau, \\ 0 & \text{if } |v| \leq \tau, \\ v + \tau & \text{if } v < -\tau. \end{cases}\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $p_i = S_{\eta\lambda}(v_i)$. By the criterion above and Proposition~\ref{prop:subgradients}, we must check that $\frac{1}{\eta}(v_i - p_i) \in \lambda\,\partial|\cdot|(p_i)$ for every $i$. If $v_i > \eta\lambda$, then $p_i > 0$ and $\frac{1}{\eta}(v_i - p_i) = \lambda = \lambda\sign(p_i)$; the case $v_i < -\eta\lambda$ is symmetric. If $|v_i| \leq \eta\lambda$, then $p_i = 0$ and $\frac{1}{\eta}v_i \in [-\lambda, \lambda]$.
\end{proof}

The proximal gradient method takes a gradient step for the smooth part and a proximal step for the nonsmooth part:
\[\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}\big(\mathbf{x}_k - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)\big).\]
For the lasso, this is the iterative soft-thresholding algorithm (ISTA), $\mathbf{w}_{k+1} = S_{\eta\lambda}\big(\mathbf{w}_k - \eta X^\top(X\mathbf{w}_k - \mathbf{y})\big)$, applied entrywise. For $h = 0$, it is gradient descent.

\begin{theorem}[Proximal gradient method]\label{thm:prox-gradient}
    Let $f$ be convex and $L$-smooth, let $h$ be convex, let $F = f + h$, let $0 < \eta \leq \frac{1}{L}$, and suppose that the proximal steps exist.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\mathbf{x}^*$ minimizes $F$ if and only if $\mathbf{x}^* = \operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}\big(\mathbf{x}^* - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)\big)$.
        \item The method decreases $F$: $F(\mathbf{x}_{k+1}) \leq F(\mathbf{x}_k) - \frac{1}{2\eta}\norm{\mathbf{x}_{k+1} - \mathbf{x}_k}^2$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    (1) By the criterion for the proximal operator, $\mathbf{x}^* = \operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}(\mathbf{x}^* - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*))$ if and only if $\frac{1}{\eta}\big(\mathbf{x}^* - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) - \mathbf{x}^*\big) = -\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) \in \partial h(\mathbf{x}^*)$, which is the condition of Theorem~\ref{thm:composite-optimality}.

    (2) Let $\mathbf{p} = \mathbf{x}_{k+1}$ and $\mathbf{d} = \mathbf{p} - \mathbf{x}_k$. By the criterion, $\mathbf{g} = \frac{1}{\eta}\big(\mathbf{x}_k - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k) - \mathbf{p}\big) = -\frac{1}{\eta}\mathbf{d} - \nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)$ is a subgradient of $h$ at $\mathbf{p}$, so $h(\mathbf{x}_k) \geq h(\mathbf{p}) - \mathbf{g}^\top\mathbf{d}$. By the descent lemma, $f(\mathbf{p}) \leq f(\mathbf{x}_k) + \nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)^\top\mathbf{d} + \frac{L}{2}\norm{\mathbf{d}}^2$. Adding,
    \[F(\mathbf{p}) \leq F(\mathbf{x}_k) + \big(\mathbf{g} + \nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)\big)^\top\mathbf{d} + \frac{L}{2}\norm{\mathbf{d}}^2 = F(\mathbf{x}_k) - \Big(\frac{1}{\eta} - \frac{L}{2}\Big)\norm{\mathbf{d}}^2,\]
    and $\frac{1}{\eta} - \frac{L}{2} \geq \frac{1}{2\eta}$ since $\eta L \leq 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Why the lasso gives sparse weights]\label{ex:lasso}
    Suppose that the columns of $X$ are orthonormal, $X^\top X = I$. Then $\frac{1}{2}\norm{X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}}^2 = \frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{w} - X^\top\mathbf{y}}^2 + c$, where $c = \frac{1}{2}(\norm{\mathbf{y}}^2 - \norm{X^\top\mathbf{y}}^2)$ does not depend on $\mathbf{w}$ (expand both sides). So the lasso solution is $\mathbf{w}^* = \operatorname{prox}_{\lambda\norm{\cdot}_1}(X^\top\mathbf{y})$, i.e., $w_i^* = S_\lambda\big((X^\top\mathbf{y})_i\big)$: the least squares weights $(X^\top\mathbf{y})_i$ are shrunk towards $0$ by $\lambda$, and those with $|(X^\top\mathbf{y})_i| \leq \lambda$ become exactly $0$. For example, least squares weights $(3, -0.5, 1.2, 0.1)$ with $\lambda = 1$ give the lasso weights $(2, 0, 0.2, 0)$. Ridge regression instead gives $\frac{1}{1 + 2\lambda}(X^\top\mathbf{y})$ for the penalty $\lambda\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2$, which shrinks all weights by the same factor, but sets none of them to $0$.
\end{example}

Other proximal operators are just as simple. For the indicator function of a closed convex set $C$ (which is $0$ on $C$ and $+\infty$ outside, so we have to allow the value $+\infty$), the proximal operator is the orthogonal projection onto $C$, and the proximal gradient method becomes projected gradient descent (Example~\ref{ex:kkt-ball}). Automatic differentiation libraries use subgradients as well: for $\ReLU$ at $0$, they return a fixed element of $\partial\ReLU(0) = [0, 1]$, usually $0$.

\section{Constrained Optimization}\label{sec:constrained}

Many optimization problems in machine learning have constraints. In principal component analysis, we maximize the variance over unit vectors; a vector of probabilities must have nonnegative entries that sum to $1$; and support vector machines maximize a margin subject to classifying all training points correctly. We first consider equality constraints:
\[\text{minimize } f(\mathbf{x}) \quad \text{subject to} \quad g_1(\mathbf{x}) = 0, \ \ldots, \ g_m(\mathbf{x}) = 0.\]

\subsection{Lagrange multipliers}

Consider first one constraint $g(\mathbf{x}) = 0$ in $\mathbb{R}^2$, which is usually a curve. If we walk along the curve, then at a local minimum of $f$ on the curve, the derivative of $f$ in the direction of the curve must be zero, i.e., $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)$ is orthogonal to the curve. By Corollary~\ref{cor:level-sets}, $\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^*)$ is also orthogonal to the curve, which is a level set of $g$. In the plane, this means that $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)$ and $\nabla g(\mathbf{x}^*)$ are parallel: the level curve of $f$ through $\mathbf{x}^*$ touches the constraint curve (Figure~\ref{fig:lagrange}). At a point where $\nabla f$ is not orthogonal to the curve, we can decrease $f$ by moving along the curve.

\begin{theorem}[Lagrange multipliers]\label{thm:lagrange}
    Let $f, g_1, \ldots, g_m: D \to \mathbb{R}$ be continuously differentiable on an open set $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$, and let $\mathbf{x}^*$ be a local minimum of $f$ on the set $S = \{\mathbf{x} \in D : g_1(\mathbf{x}) = \cdots = g_m(\mathbf{x}) = 0\}$. If the gradients $\nabla g_1(\mathbf{x}^*), \ldots, \nabla g_m(\mathbf{x}^*)$ are linearly independent, then there are unique numbers $\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_m \in \mathbb{R}$, called Lagrange multipliers, such that
    \[\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) + \lambda_1\nabla g_1(\mathbf{x}^*) + \cdots + \lambda_m\nabla g_m(\mathbf{x}^*) = \mathbf{0}.\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Let $U = \Span\{\nabla g_1(\mathbf{x}^*), \ldots, \nabla g_m(\mathbf{x}^*)\}$. If $\mathbf{x}(t)$ is a differentiable curve in $S$ with $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}^*$, then $g_i(\mathbf{x}(t)) = 0$ for all $t$, so $\nabla g_i(\mathbf{x}^*)^\top\mathbf{x}'(0) = 0$ by the chain rule, i.e., the tangent vector $\mathbf{x}'(0)$ lies in $U^\perp$. Conversely, the implicit function theorem from analysis, which we do not prove, shows that, since the $\nabla g_i(\mathbf{x}^*)$ are linearly independent, every vector $\mathbf{v} \in U^\perp$ is the tangent vector $\mathbf{x}'(0)$ of such a curve. Since $t \mapsto f(\mathbf{x}(t))$ has a local minimum at $t = 0$, its derivative $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)^\top\mathbf{x}'(0)$ is $0$. Hence $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)$ is orthogonal to every vector in $U^\perp$, i.e., $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) \in (U^\perp)^\perp = U$ (Lecture~2). So $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*)$ is a linear combination of the $\nabla g_i(\mathbf{x}^*)$, and the coefficients are unique, since the $\nabla g_i(\mathbf{x}^*)$ are linearly independent.
\end{proof}

The same condition holds at local maxima (apply the theorem to $-f$; the multipliers can have any sign). It is convenient to write it with the Lagrangian
\[\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\lambda}) = f(\mathbf{x}) + \lambda_1 g_1(\mathbf{x}) + \cdots + \lambda_m g_m(\mathbf{x}).\]
The condition of the theorem says that $\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\lambda}) = \mathbf{0}$, and the constraints say that $\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\lambda}) = (g_1(\mathbf{x}^*), \ldots, g_m(\mathbf{x}^*))^\top = \mathbf{0}$. So we look for critical points of $\mathcal{L}$, which gives $n + m$ equations for the $n + m$ unknowns $\mathbf{x}$ and $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$. If $S$ is closed and bounded (such as a sphere), then $f$ attains its minimum and maximum on $S$ (this is proved in the separate appendix on topology, differential topology and functional analysis), and we find them by comparing the values of $f$ at all solutions of these equations.

\begin{example}\label{ex:lagrange-circle}
    We find the maximum and minimum of $f(x, y) = x + y$ on the unit circle $g(x, y) = x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$. The condition $\nabla f + \lambda\nabla g = \mathbf{0}$ is $(1, 1) + \lambda(2x, 2y) = (0, 0)$, so $x = y = -\frac{1}{2\lambda}$, and with $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, we get $x = y = \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$. The maximum is $f(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}) = \sqrt{2}$, and the minimum is $-\sqrt{2}$. This agrees with the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality: $x + y = (1, 1)(x, y)^\top \leq \sqrt{2}\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} = \sqrt{2}$. See Figure~\ref{fig:lagrange}.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.5, >=Stealth]
        \begin{scope}
            \clip (-1.9,-1.6) rectangle (2.1,1.9);
            \foreach \c in {-2,-1,0,1,2}
                \draw[gray, dashed] ({\c/2 - 2},{\c/2 + 2}) -- ({\c/2 + 2},{\c/2 - 2});
            \draw[orange!85!black, thick] ({sqrt(2)/2 - 2},{sqrt(2)/2 + 2}) -- ({sqrt(2)/2 + 2},{sqrt(2)/2 - 2});
        \end{scope}
        \draw[->, gray] (-1.8,0) -- (2.0,0) node[right] {$x$};
        \draw[->, gray] (0,-1.5) -- (0,1.8) node[above] {$y$};
        \draw[blue!70!black, thick] (0,0) circle (1);
        \coordinate (P) at (45:1);
        \draw[->, very thick, blue!70!black] (P) -- ++(0.5,0.5) node[above right] {$\nabla g$};
        \draw[->, thick, red!75!black] (P) -- ++(0.32,0.32) node[above left] {$\nabla f$};
        \fill (P) circle (1.3pt) node[below left] {$\mathbf{x}^*$};
        \coordinate (Q) at (160:1);
        \draw[->, thick, red!75!black] (Q) -- ++(0.32,0.32);
        \draw[->, very thick, blue!70!black] (Q) -- ++({0.5*cos(160)},{0.5*sin(160)});
        \fill (Q) circle (1.3pt);
        \node[orange!85!black, font=\small, fill=white, inner sep=1pt] at (1.85,-0.85) {$x + y = \sqrt{2}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{Maximizing $f(x, y) = x + y$ on the unit circle $g(x, y) = x^2 + y^2 - 1 = 0$. The dashed lines are level lines of $f$. At the maximum $\mathbf{x}^*$, the level line $x + y = \sqrt{2}$ touches the circle, and $\nabla f$ and $\nabla g$ are parallel. At the other marked point, they are not parallel: $\nabla f$ has a component along the circle, so moving along the circle in that direction increases $f$.}
    \label{fig:lagrange}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Eigenvalues and PCA]\label{ex:lagrange-eigen}
    Let $A$ be a symmetric $n \times n$ matrix. We maximize $f(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ subject to $g(\mathbf{x}) = 1 - \mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{x} = 0$. Here $\nabla g(\mathbf{x}) = -2\mathbf{x} \neq \mathbf{0}$ on the unit sphere, and the Lagrange condition $2A\mathbf{x} - 2\lambda\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}$ says that
    \[A\mathbf{x} = \lambda\mathbf{x}:\]
    the candidates are exactly the unit eigenvectors of $A$, and the Lagrange multiplier is the eigenvalue. At a unit eigenvector, $f(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^\top(\lambda\mathbf{x}) = \lambda$. Since the sphere is closed and bounded, the maximum exists, and it is the largest eigenvalue $\lambda_{\max}$, attained at a corresponding eigenvector. This is the Rayleigh quotient result of Lecture~2, found with calculus instead of the spectral theorem; in PCA, it gives the first principal component.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[The minimum norm solution]\label{ex:min-norm}
    Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ have linearly independent rows $\mathbf{a}_1^\top, \ldots, \mathbf{a}_m^\top$ (so $m \leq n$), and let $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^m$. The system $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ has infinitely many solutions if $m < n$, and we look for the one with the smallest norm: minimize $\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2$ subject to $g_i(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{a}_i^\top\mathbf{x} - b_i = 0$ for $i = 1, \ldots, m$. The gradients $\nabla g_i = \mathbf{a}_i$ are linearly independent, and the Lagrange condition is
    \[2\mathbf{x} + \sum_{i=1}^m \lambda_i\mathbf{a}_i = 2\mathbf{x} + A^\top\boldsymbol{\lambda} = \mathbf{0}, \qquad \text{so} \qquad \mathbf{x} = A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} \text{ with } \boldsymbol{\mu} = -\tfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\lambda}.\]
    So $\mathbf{x}$ lies in the row space of $A$. Substituting into $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ gives $AA^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} = \mathbf{b}$. The $m \times m$ matrix $AA^\top$ is invertible (it is positive definite, since $\mathbf{v}^\top AA^\top\mathbf{v} = \norm{A^\top\mathbf{v}}^2 > 0$ for $\mathbf{v} \neq \mathbf{0}$), so
    \[\mathbf{x}^* = A^\top(AA^\top)^{-1}\mathbf{b}.\]
    This is indeed the global minimum: every solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ is $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}^* + \mathbf{v}$ with $\mathbf{v} \in \Null(A)$, and since $\mathbf{x}^* \in \row(A) = \Null(A)^\perp$, Pythagoras gives $\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2 = \norm{\mathbf{x}^*}^2 + \norm{\mathbf{v}}^2 \geq \norm{\mathbf{x}^*}^2$. By the proposition on the pseudo-inverse in Lecture~2, applied to $A^\top$ (which has linearly independent columns), $(A^\top)^+ = (AA^\top)^{-1}A$. By the definition of the pseudo-inverse with the SVD, $(A^\top)^+ = (A^+)^\top$, so taking transposes gives $A^+ = A^\top(AA^\top)^{-1}$. So $\mathbf{x}^* = A^+\mathbf{b}$, which gives a second proof, for matrices with linearly independent rows, of the result of Lecture~2 that $A^+\mathbf{b}$ is the solution with the smallest norm.
\end{example}

\subsection{Inequality constraints and the KKT conditions}

Now consider inequality constraints:
\[\text{minimize } f(\mathbf{x}) \quad \text{subject to} \quad g_1(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0, \ \ldots, \ g_m(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0.\]
At a feasible point $\mathbf{x}^*$, the constraint $g_i$ is active if $g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) = 0$, and inactive if $g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) < 0$. Inactive constraints do not matter near $\mathbf{x}^*$, since they remain satisfied when we move $\mathbf{x}^*$ a little. Active constraints behave like equality constraints, with one difference: moving into the feasible region, where $g_i < 0$, is allowed. At a minimum, this must not decrease $f$, and this forces the multipliers to be nonnegative.

\begin{theorem}[Karush--Kuhn--Tucker conditions]\label{thm:kkt}
    Let $f, g_1, \ldots, g_m$ be continuously differentiable, and let $\mathbf{x}^*$ be a local minimum of $f$ subject to $g_1(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0, \ldots, g_m(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0$, such that the gradients of the active constraints at $\mathbf{x}^*$ are linearly independent. Then there are numbers $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_m$ such that:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item (stationarity) $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}^*) + \mu_1\nabla g_1(\mathbf{x}^*) + \cdots + \mu_m\nabla g_m(\mathbf{x}^*) = \mathbf{0}$;
        \item (primal feasibility) $g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) \leq 0$ for all $i$;
        \item (dual feasibility) $\mu_i \geq 0$ for all $i$;
        \item (complementary slackness) $\mu_i g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) = 0$ for all $i$, i.e., $\mu_i = 0$ for every inactive constraint.
    \end{enumerate}
    Conversely, if $f$ and all $g_i$ are convex and differentiable, then every $\mathbf{x}^*$ that satisfies (1)--(4) for some $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_m$ is a global minimum.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of the converse]
    The first part is proved in the separate appendix on topology, differential topology and functional analysis, with the implicit function theorem. For the converse, let $\mathbf{x}$ be feasible. The function $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{z}) = f(\mathbf{z}) + \sum_i \mu_i g_i(\mathbf{z})$ is convex by Proposition~\ref{prop:convex-operations}, since $\mu_i \geq 0$, and $\nabla\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*) = \mathbf{0}$ by (1). By Corollary~\ref{cor:convex-global}, $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}) \geq \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*)$. Since $\mu_i g_i(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0$ for feasible $\mathbf{x}$ and $\mu_i g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) = 0$ by (4),
    \[f(\mathbf{x}) \geq f(\mathbf{x}) + \sum_i \mu_i g_i(\mathbf{x}) = \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}) \geq \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*) = f(\mathbf{x}^*).\qedhere\]
\end{proof}

Equality constraints can be added to the KKT conditions with multipliers of any sign, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:lagrange}.

\begin{example}\label{ex:kkt-1d}
    Minimize $f(x) = (x - 2)^2$ subject to $x \leq 1$, i.e., $g(x) = x - 1 \leq 0$. The KKT conditions are $2(x - 2) + \mu = 0$, $x \leq 1$, $\mu \geq 0$ and $\mu(x - 1) = 0$. If $\mu = 0$, then $x = 2$, which is not feasible. So the constraint is active, $x = 1$, and $\mu = 2 \geq 0$. Since $f$ and $g$ are convex, $x^* = 1$ is the global minimum. The multiplier measures how much the constraint costs: if we relax it to $x \leq 1 + \varepsilon$, the minimum value $(1 + \varepsilon - 2)^2 = 1 - 2\varepsilon + \varepsilon^2$ decreases at the rate $\mu = 2$. For the constraint $x \leq 3$ instead, the minimum $x^* = 2$ satisfies the constraint, which is inactive, and $\mu = 0$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Projection onto a ball]\label{ex:kkt-ball}
    Let $\mathbf{p} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ with $\norm{\mathbf{p}} > 1$. We find the point of the unit ball closest to $\mathbf{p}$: minimize $\norm{\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{p}}^2$ subject to $\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2 - 1 \leq 0$. Stationarity is $2(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{p}) + 2\mu\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}$, i.e., $\mathbf{x} = \frac{\mathbf{p}}{1 + \mu}$. If $\mu = 0$, then $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{p}$ is not feasible, so the constraint is active, $\norm{\mathbf{x}} = 1$, which gives $1 + \mu = \norm{\mathbf{p}}$, $\mu = \norm{\mathbf{p}} - 1 > 0$, and
    \[\mathbf{x}^* = \frac{\mathbf{p}}{\norm{\mathbf{p}}}.\]
    For example, for $\mathbf{p} = (3, 4)^\top$, we get $\mathbf{x}^* = (0.6, 0.8)^\top$ and $\mu = 4$. This projection is used in projected gradient descent, which alternates gradient steps with projections onto the feasible set.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Support vector machines]
    For linearly separable data $(\mathbf{x}_i, y_i)$ with labels $y_i = \pm 1$, the support vector machine finds the separating hyperplane $\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x} + b = 0$ with the largest margin. By the formula for the distance to a hyperplane in Lecture~2, this leads to the convex problem
    \[\text{minimize } \tfrac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2 \quad \text{subject to} \quad 1 - y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x}_i + b) \leq 0 \quad \text{for } i = 1, \ldots, N,\]
    whose margin is $\frac{1}{\norm{\mathbf{w}}}$. The stationarity condition gives $\mathbf{w} = \sum_i \mu_i y_i\mathbf{x}_i$, and by complementary slackness, $\mu_i > 0$ only for the points with $y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x}_i + b) = 1$, which lie exactly on the margin. These points are the support vectors: the classifier only depends on them.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Lagrangian duality}\label{sec:duality}

The multipliers of the KKT conditions are more than auxiliary variables: they solve an optimization problem of their own. Consider again the problem
\[\text{minimize } f(\mathbf{x}) \quad \text{subject to} \quad g_1(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0, \ \ldots, \ g_m(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0,\]
with the optimal value $p^* = \inf\{f(\mathbf{x}) : \mathbf{x} \text{ feasible}\}$, the primal problem.

\begin{definition}[Dual problem]\label{def:dual}
    The Lagrangian of the problem is $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}) = f(\mathbf{x}) + \sum_{i=1}^m \mu_ig_i(\mathbf{x})$, the dual function is
    \[d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) = \inf_{\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}) \qquad \text{for } \boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}\]
    (which may be $-\infty$), and the dual problem is to maximize $d(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ subject to $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$, i.e., $\mu_i \geq 0$ for all $i$, with the optimal value $d^* = \sup_{\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}} d(\boldsymbol{\mu})$.
\end{definition}

For each fixed $\mathbf{x}$, $\boldsymbol{\mu} \mapsto \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu})$ is affine, and an infimum of affine functions is concave: if $\boldsymbol{\mu} = \theta\boldsymbol{\mu}_1 + (1 - \theta)\boldsymbol{\mu}_2$, then $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}) = \theta\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}_1) + (1 - \theta)\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}_2) \geq \theta d(\boldsymbol{\mu}_1) + (1 - \theta)d(\boldsymbol{\mu}_2)$ for every $\mathbf{x}$. So the dual problem is always a concave maximization problem, even if the primal problem is not convex.

\begin{theorem}[Weak and strong duality]\label{thm:duality}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item (Weak duality.) $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) \leq f(\mathbf{x})$ for every feasible $\mathbf{x}$ and every $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$. Hence $d^* \leq p^*$.
        \item (Strong duality.) Let $f, g_1, \ldots, g_m$ be convex and differentiable, and let $\mathbf{x}^*$ and $\boldsymbol{\mu}^*$ satisfy the KKT conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:kkt}. Then $\mathbf{x}^*$ solves the primal problem, $\boldsymbol{\mu}^*$ solves the dual problem, and $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}^*) = f(\mathbf{x}^*)$, so $d^* = p^*$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    (1) For feasible $\mathbf{x}$, $\mu_ig_i(\mathbf{x}) \leq 0$, so $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) \leq \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu}) \leq f(\mathbf{x})$.

    (2) The function $\mathcal{L}(\cdot, \boldsymbol{\mu}^*)$ is convex (Proposition~\ref{prop:convex-operations}, since $\mu_i^* \geq 0$), and its gradient at $\mathbf{x}^*$ is $\mathbf{0}$ by stationarity, so $\mathbf{x}^*$ minimizes it (Corollary~\ref{cor:convex-global}). By complementary slackness, $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}^*) = \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\mu}^*) = f(\mathbf{x}^*)$. By weak duality, $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) \leq f(\mathbf{x}^*) = d(\boldsymbol{\mu}^*)$ for all $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$, and $f(\mathbf{x}) \geq d(\boldsymbol{\mu}^*) = f(\mathbf{x}^*)$ for all feasible $\mathbf{x}$.
\end{proof}

Together with Theorem~\ref{thm:kkt}, strong duality holds for every convex problem whose solution has linearly independent gradients of the active constraints. Duality is useful in several ways. Every $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$ gives a lower bound $d(\boldsymbol{\mu})$ for the optimal value, so the duality gap $f(\mathbf{x}) - d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) \geq f(\mathbf{x}) - p^*$ certifies how far a feasible point $\mathbf{x}$ is from being optimal; solvers stop when it is small. The dual problem may also be easier, for example when it has fewer variables, or simpler constraints.

\begin{example}\label{ex:dual-1d}
    For the problem of Example~\ref{ex:kkt-1d}, minimize $(x - 2)^2$ subject to $x - 1 \leq 0$, the Lagrangian $(x - 2)^2 + \mu(x - 1)$ is smallest at $x = 2 - \frac{\mu}{2}$, so $d(\mu) = \frac{\mu^2}{4} + \mu\big(1 - \frac{\mu}{2}\big) = \mu - \frac{\mu^2}{4}$. The concave function $d$ is largest at $\mu = 2$, with $d^* = 1 = p^*$, and the maximizer is the KKT multiplier $\mu = 2$ found there.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[The dual of the support vector machine]\label{ex:svm-dual}
    For the support vector machine of the remark above, with the variables $\mathbf{w}$ and $b$, the Lagrangian is
    \[\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{w}, b, \boldsymbol{\mu}) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2 + \sum_{i=1}^N \mu_i\big(1 - y_i(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x}_i + b)\big) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2 - \mathbf{w}^\top\sum_i \mu_iy_i\mathbf{x}_i - b\sum_i \mu_iy_i + \sum_i \mu_i.\]
    As a function of $b$, it is affine, so its infimum over $b$ is $-\infty$ unless $\sum_i \mu_iy_i = 0$. As a function of $\mathbf{w}$, it is a convex quadratic function, which is smallest where its gradient $\mathbf{w} - \sum_i \mu_iy_i\mathbf{x}_i$ vanishes. Substituting $\mathbf{w} = \sum_i \mu_iy_i\mathbf{x}_i$ gives the dual problem
    \[\text{maximize } \sum_{i=1}^N \mu_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i, j = 1}^N \mu_i\mu_jy_iy_j\,\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{x}_j \quad \text{subject to} \quad \boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}, \quad \sum_{i=1}^N \mu_iy_i = 0.\]
    Whenever the KKT conditions hold at the solution, Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} shows that solving the dual problem solves the support vector machine, with $\mathbf{w} = \sum_i \mu_iy_i\mathbf{x}_i$. The dual has one variable per data point instead of per feature, and it only depends on the data through the inner products $\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{x}_j$. Replacing them by a kernel $k(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)$ (the remark on kernels in Lecture~2) gives the kernel support vector machine, which separates the data by a nonlinear boundary.

    For example, for the two points $\mathbf{x}_1 = (1, 0)^\top$ with $y_1 = 1$ and $\mathbf{x}_2 = (-1, 0)^\top$ with $y_2 = -1$, the constraint $\mu_1 - \mu_2 = 0$ gives $\mu_1 = \mu_2 = m$, and $\sum_i \mu_iy_i\mathbf{x}_i = (2m, 0)^\top$, so the dual function is $2m - 2m^2$. It is largest at $m = \frac{1}{2}$, with the value $\frac{1}{2}$. Then $\mathbf{w} = (1, 0)^\top$, and $b = 0$ from the active constraint $y_1(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x}_1 + b) = 1$. The separating line is $x_1 = 0$, with margin $\frac{1}{\norm{\mathbf{w}}} = 1$, and the primal value $\frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2 = \frac{1}{2}$ equals the dual value.
\end{example}

For problems with linear constraints, the dual function can be written with the following transform of $f$.

\begin{definition}[Convex conjugate]\label{def:conjugate}
    The convex conjugate of $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ is the function
    \[f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \sup_{\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n} \big(\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})\big) \qquad (\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^n),\]
    which may take the value $+\infty$.
\end{definition}

So $f^*(\mathbf{y})$ is the largest amount by which the linear function $\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x}$ exceeds $f$. For $n = 1$: the line with slope $y$ that touches the graph of $f$ from below crosses the vertical axis at $-f^*(y)$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:conjugate}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The set where $f^*$ is finite is convex, and $f^*$ is convex on it.
        \item (Fenchel--Young inequality.) $f(\mathbf{x}) + f^*(\mathbf{y}) \geq \mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{y}$ for all $\mathbf{x}$ and $\mathbf{y}$.
        \item If $f$ is convex and differentiable, then $f^*(\nabla f(\mathbf{x})) = \mathbf{x}^\top\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) - f(\mathbf{x})$ for every $\mathbf{x}$, and $f^{**} = f$, i.e., $f(\mathbf{x}) = \sup_{\mathbf{y}}\big(\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{y} - f^*(\mathbf{y})\big)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    (1) If $f^*(\mathbf{y}_1)$ and $f^*(\mathbf{y}_2)$ are finite and $\theta \in [0, 1]$, then for every $\mathbf{x}$,
    \[\big(\theta\mathbf{y}_1 + (1 - \theta)\mathbf{y}_2\big)^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x}) = \theta\big(\mathbf{y}_1^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})\big) + (1 - \theta)\big(\mathbf{y}_2^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})\big) \leq \theta f^*(\mathbf{y}_1) + (1 - \theta)f^*(\mathbf{y}_2),\]
    and the supremum over $\mathbf{x}$ gives $f^*(\theta\mathbf{y}_1 + (1 - \theta)\mathbf{y}_2) \leq \theta f^*(\mathbf{y}_1) + (1 - \theta)f^*(\mathbf{y}_2) < \infty$.

    (2) $\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x}) \leq f^*(\mathbf{y})$ by the definition of the supremum.

    (3) Let $\mathbf{y} = \nabla f(\mathbf{x})$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:convex-first-order}, $f(\mathbf{z}) \geq f(\mathbf{x}) + \mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{z} - \mathbf{x})$, i.e., $\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{z} - f(\mathbf{z}) \leq \mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{z}$, with equality for $\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{x}$; so $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})$. Hence $\sup_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{y} - f^*(\mathbf{y})) \geq \mathbf{x}^\top\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) - f^*(\nabla f(\mathbf{x})) = f(\mathbf{x})$, and the reverse inequality is (2).
\end{proof}

Part 3 says that a convex differentiable function is the supremum of its tangent functions $\mathbf{x} \mapsto f(\mathbf{x}_0) + \nabla f(\mathbf{x}_0)^\top(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_0)$, and that $f^*$ records the tangents by their slopes: $f$ and $f^*$ contain the same information. (This is the Legendre transform of physics.)

\begin{example}\label{ex:conjugates}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top Q\mathbf{x}$ with $Q$ symmetric positive definite, the supremum of $\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top Q\mathbf{x}$ is attained where $\mathbf{y} = Q\mathbf{x}$, so $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{y}^\top Q^{-1}\mathbf{y}$. In particular, $\frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2$ is its own conjugate.
        \item For $f(x) = e^x$, $xy - e^x$ is largest at $x = \log y$ if $y > 0$, so $f^*(y) = y\log y - y$; $f^*(0) = 0$; and $f^*(y) = \infty$ for $y < 0$, since $xy - e^x \to \infty$ as $x \to -\infty$.
        \item For $f(\mathbf{x}) = \norm{\mathbf{x}}_1$: if $|y_i| \leq 1$ for all $i$, then $\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - \norm{\mathbf{x}}_1 = \sum_i (y_ix_i - |x_i|) \leq 0$, with equality for $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}$, so $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = 0$; if $|y_j| > 1$, then $\mathbf{x} = t\,\sign(y_j)\mathbf{e}_j$ gives $t(|y_j| - 1) \to \infty$. So $f^*$ is $0$ on the unit ball of the $\ell_\infty$ norm and $\infty$ outside it.
        \item For the log-sum-exp function $\operatorname{lse}(\mathbf{x}) = \log(e^{x_1} + \cdots + e^{x_n})$, which is convex with the gradient $\softmax(\mathbf{x})$: if $\mathbf{p}$ has positive entries with sum $1$, then $\softmax(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{p}$ for $x_i = \log p_i$, and part 3 gives $\operatorname{lse}^*(\mathbf{p}) = \sum_i p_i\log p_i - \log\sum_i p_i = \sum_i p_i\log p_i$, the negative entropy of the distribution $\mathbf{p}$ (Lecture~4).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

Now consider the problem of minimizing $f(\mathbf{x})$ subject to $A\mathbf{x} \leq \mathbf{b}$ (i.e., $g_i(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{a}_i^\top\mathbf{x} - b_i \leq 0$ for the rows $\mathbf{a}_i^\top$ of $A$). Its dual function is
\[d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) = \inf_{\mathbf{x}}\big(f(\mathbf{x}) + \boldsymbol{\mu}^\top(A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b})\big) = -\mathbf{b}^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} - \sup_{\mathbf{x}}\big((-A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu})^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x})\big) = -\mathbf{b}^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} - f^*(-A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu}).\]

\begin{example}[Linear and quadratic programs]\label{ex:lp-qp}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item A linear program minimizes $f(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{c}^\top\mathbf{x}$ subject to $A\mathbf{x} \leq \mathbf{b}$. Here $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \sup_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{c})^\top\mathbf{x}$ is $0$ for $\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{c}$ and $\infty$ otherwise. So the dual problem is again a linear program: maximize $-\mathbf{b}^\top\boldsymbol{\mu}$ subject to $A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} = -\mathbf{c}$ and $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$. For example, minimize $-x_1 - x_2$ subject to $0 \leq x_1 \leq 1$, $0 \leq x_2 \leq 1$ and $x_1 + x_2 \leq 1.5$. The optimal value is $-1.5$, attained at $(1, 0.5)$, for example. The dual point with the multiplier $1$ for $x_1 + x_2 \leq 1.5$ and $0$ for the other constraints is feasible, with the dual value $-1.5$: it is a certificate of optimality, since adding the constraint $x_1 + x_2 \leq 1.5$ with the weight $1$ proves $-x_1 - x_2 \geq -1.5$ for every feasible $\mathbf{x}$.
        \item A quadratic program minimizes $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top Q\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{c}^\top\mathbf{x}$ with $Q$ symmetric positive definite, subject to $A\mathbf{x} \leq \mathbf{b}$. As in Example~\ref{ex:conjugates}, $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{c})^\top Q^{-1}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{c})$, so the dual problem is to maximize $-\mathbf{b}^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} - \frac{1}{2}(A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} + \mathbf{c})^\top Q^{-1}(A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} + \mathbf{c})$ subject to $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$. It has only the simple constraints $\mu_i \geq 0$, which projected gradient ascent handles by setting negative entries to $0$. The dual of the support vector machine is of this kind.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Constraint qualifications]
    Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} needs a point that satisfies the KKT conditions, which Theorem~\ref{thm:kkt} guarantees when the gradients of the active constraints are linearly independent. Strong duality holds more generally: for linear programs whenever $p^*$ is finite, and for convex problems that have a strictly feasible point, with $g_i(\bar{\mathbf{x}}) < 0$ for all $i$ (Slater's condition). Both facts are proved with separating hyperplanes, and we do not prove them here.
\end{remark}

\section{Vector Fields: Divergence and Curl}\label{sec:vector-fields}

\begin{definition}[Vector field]\label{def:vector-field}
    A vector field on an open set $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$ is a function $F: D \to \mathbb{R}^n$. We picture it by attaching to every point $\mathbf{x} \in D$ the arrow $F(\mathbf{x})$, starting at $\mathbf{x}$.
\end{definition}

The most important examples for us are gradient fields: the gradient $\nabla f$ of a differentiable function $f: D \to \mathbb{R}$ is a vector field, whose arrows point in the direction of steepest ascent of $f$ and are orthogonal to its level sets (Section~\ref{sec:gradients}). Another example is the velocity field of a fluid, which tells us how fast and in which direction a particle at $\mathbf{x}$ moves. This motion is described by a differential equation, the subject of Section~\ref{sec:ode}.

The derivative of a vector field is a square matrix, the Jacobian $DF(\mathbf{x}) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, and in this section we extract two geometric quantities from it: the divergence, which measures how much of $F$ flows out of small regions, and the curl, which measures how $F$ rotates.

\begin{example}\label{ex:vector-fields}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The gradient field of $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2$ is $F(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}$, which points away from the origin. For a symmetric matrix $A$, the field $A\mathbf{x}$ is the gradient field of $\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:basic-gradients}).
        \item The rotation field $F(x, y) = (-y, x)^\top$ is orthogonal to the position vector $(x, y)^\top$ everywhere: its arrows go around the origin counterclockwise (Figure~\ref{fig:vector-fields}).
        \item Let $p$ be a positive probability density on $\mathbb{R}^n$. Its score is the vector field $\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{x}) = \nabla\log p(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\nabla p(\mathbf{x})}{p(\mathbf{x})}$, which points in the direction in which the density increases fastest. For the Gaussian density $p(\mathbf{x}) \propto \exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})^\top\Sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})\big)$ of Lecture~4, $\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{x}) = -\Sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu})$ points towards the mean. Score-based generative models, such as diffusion models, learn the score of the distribution of the training data with a neural network $\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ (Lecture~4).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

Near a point $\mathbf{x}$, $F(\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{h}) \approx F(\mathbf{x}) + DF(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{h}$, and every square matrix is the sum of a symmetric and a skew-symmetric matrix:
\[DF(\mathbf{x}) = S + W, \qquad S = \tfrac{1}{2}\big(DF(\mathbf{x}) + DF(\mathbf{x})^\top\big), \qquad W = \tfrac{1}{2}\big(DF(\mathbf{x}) - DF(\mathbf{x})^\top\big).\]
By the spectral theorem (Lecture~2), $S$ has an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors: the symmetric part stretches or compresses a small neighborhood of $\mathbf{x}$ along these orthogonal directions, at rates given by the eigenvalues. The skew-symmetric part satisfies $\mathbf{h}^\top W\mathbf{h} = 0$, so $W\mathbf{h}$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf{h}$: it is an infinitesimal rotation. The divergence summarizes the symmetric part, and the curl is the skew-symmetric part.

\begin{definition}[Divergence]\label{def:divergence}
    The divergence of a differentiable vector field $F$ is the function
    \[\Div F(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_1}(\mathbf{x}) + \cdots + \frac{\partial F_n}{\partial x_n}(\mathbf{x}) = \tr DF(\mathbf{x}).\]
    It is also written $\nabla \cdot F$, as a formal dot product of $\nabla = (\frac{\partial}{\partial x_1}, \ldots, \frac{\partial}{\partial x_n})^\top$ with $F$.
\end{definition}

Since $\tr W = 0$, the divergence only depends on the symmetric part: $\Div F(\mathbf{x}) = \tr S$ is the sum of the stretching rates. To see what it measures, think of $F$ as the velocity field of a fluid, and consider a small box $Q$ around $\mathbf{x}$ with the side lengths $h_1, \ldots, h_n$. The two faces of $Q$ perpendicular to $\mathbf{e}_1$ have the area $\frac{\vol(Q)}{h_1}$ and the outward normals $\pm\mathbf{e}_1$, so the fluid flows out through them at the rates $F_1 \cdot \frac{\vol(Q)}{h_1}$ and $-F_1 \cdot \frac{\vol(Q)}{h_1}$, evaluated at the centers of the faces. The net outflow through the two faces is approximately
\[\Big(F_1\big(\mathbf{x} + \tfrac{h_1}{2}\mathbf{e}_1\big) - F_1\big(\mathbf{x} - \tfrac{h_1}{2}\mathbf{e}_1\big)\Big)\frac{\vol(Q)}{h_1} \approx \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_1}(\mathbf{x})\vol(Q).\]
Adding the contributions of all $n$ pairs of faces, we find that the net outflow from $Q$ is approximately $\Div F(\mathbf{x})$ times the volume of $Q$. In other words,
\begin{center}
    \emph{$\Div F(\mathbf{x})$ is the net outflow of $F$ per unit volume near $\mathbf{x}$.}
\end{center}
The divergence theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:divergence}) makes this precise. A vector field with positive divergence at $\mathbf{x}$ acts like a source there, one with negative divergence like a sink, and a field with $\Div F = 0$ describes an incompressible fluid, in which as much flows into every region as flows out. In Section~\ref{sec:flows}, we will see that the divergence is also the rate at which the flow of the differential equation $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ expands volumes.

\begin{example}\label{ex:divergence}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $F(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}$ has $\Div F = n$: the field points away from the origin and grows in length, so more flows out of every small region than flows in.
        \item The rotation field $(-y, x)^\top$ has $\Div F = 0 + 0 = 0$: the fluid circulates without being compressed.
        \item For $F(\mathbf{x}) = A\mathbf{x}$, we have $DF = A$ and $\Div F = \tr A$. For example, the field of $A = \left(\begin{smallmatrix} -2 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{smallmatrix}\right)$ has $\Div F = -4$ everywhere: it is a sink.
        \item The field $-\nabla f$, which points downhill, has $\Div(-\nabla f) = -\tr\nabla^2 f$. Near a strict local minimum, where the Hessian is positive definite, it is a sink.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{definition}[Laplacian]\label{def:laplacian}
    The Laplacian of a twice differentiable function $f: D \to \mathbb{R}$ is
    \[\Delta f = \Div\nabla f = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_1^2} + \cdots + \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_n^2} = \tr\nabla^2 f.\]
    Functions with $\Delta f = 0$ are called harmonic.
\end{definition}

The trace of the Hessian is the sum of its eigenvalues, so $\Delta f(\mathbf{x})$ is the sum of the curvatures of $f$ in the principal directions (Section~\ref{sec:hessian-eigen}), and $\Delta f \geq 0$ at a local minimum. For example, $\Delta\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2 = 2n$, while $x^2 - y^2$ is harmonic: the curvatures $2$ and $-2$ of the saddle cancel. The Laplacian also describes diffusion, such as the spreading of heat, or of the probability density of a randomly moving particle (Lecture~4).

\begin{definition}[Curl]\label{def:curl}
    The curl of a differentiable vector field $F$ on $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2$ is the function $\curl F = \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x_1} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_2}$. For $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^3$, it is the vector field
    \[\curl F = \nabla \times F = \Big(\frac{\partial F_3}{\partial x_2} - \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x_3}, \ \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_3} - \frac{\partial F_3}{\partial x_1}, \ \frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x_1} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_2}\Big)^\top.\]
\end{definition}

Up to the factor $2$, the entries of the curl are the entries $W_{ij} = \frac{1}{2}\big(\frac{\partial F_i}{\partial x_j} - \frac{\partial F_j}{\partial x_i}\big)$ of the skew-symmetric part. For $n = 2$,
\[W = \frac{\curl F(\mathbf{x})}{2}\begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix},\]
so on top of the stretching by $S$, the field rotates a small neighborhood of $\mathbf{x}$ counterclockwise with the angular velocity $\frac{1}{2}\curl F(\mathbf{x})$: a small paddle wheel in a fluid with the velocity field $F$ spins at this rate. For $n = 3$, one checks that $W\mathbf{h} = \frac{1}{2}\curl F(\mathbf{x}) \times \mathbf{h}$, a rotation about the axis $\curl F(\mathbf{x})$ with the angular velocity $\frac{1}{2}\norm{\curl F(\mathbf{x})}$. In $n$ dimensions, the skew-symmetric matrix $W$ has $\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}$ independent entries, which is $1$ for $n = 2$ and $3$ for $n = 3$; only in three dimensions can they be arranged into a vector field.

\begin{example}\label{ex:curl}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The rotation field $(-y, x)^\top$ has $\curl F = 1 - (-1) = 2$, so it rotates with the angular velocity $1$. The field $\mathbf{x}$ has $\curl F = 0$.
        \item The shear field $F(x, y) = (y, 0)^\top$ (Figure~\ref{fig:vector-fields}) has $\Div F = 0$ and $\curl F = -1$. Its Jacobian splits as
        \[\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & \frac{1}{2} \\ \frac{1}{2} & 0 \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} 0 & \frac{1}{2} \\ -\frac{1}{2} & 0 \end{pmatrix}:\]
        the shear of Lecture~1 combines a stretching along $(1, 1)$ and a compression along $(1, -1)$ (the eigenvalues $\pm\frac{1}{2}$ of $S$) with a clockwise rotation with the angular velocity $\frac{1}{2}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \newcommand{\fieldpanel}[4]{%
        \begin{scope}[shift={(#1,0)}]
            \draw[gray!60] (-1.3,0) -- (1.3,0) (0,-1.3) -- (0,1.3);
            \foreach \x in {-1,-0.5,...,1}
                \foreach \y in {-1,-0.5,...,1} {
                    \pgfmathsetmacro{\u}{0.3*(#2)}
                    \pgfmathsetmacro{\v}{0.3*(#3)}
                    \pgfmathsetmacro{\len}{abs(\u) + abs(\v)}
                    \ifdim \len pt > 0.001pt
                        \draw[->, thick, blue!70!black] (\x,\y) -- ++(\u,\v);
                    \else
                        \fill[blue!70!black] (\x,\y) circle (0.9pt);
                    \fi
                }
            \node[below, align=center, font=\small] at (0,-1.4) {#4};
        \end{scope}}
    \begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.35cm, y=1.35cm, >=Stealth]
        \fieldpanel{0}{\x}{\y}{source $F = (x, y)$\\ $\Div F = 2$, \ $\curl F = 0$}
        \fieldpanel{3.4}{0-(\y)}{\x}{rotation $F = (-y, x)$\\ $\Div F = 0$, \ $\curl F = 2$}
        \fieldpanel{6.8}{\y}{0}{shear $F = (y, 0)$\\ $\Div F = 0$, \ $\curl F = -1$}
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{Three linear vector fields. The source expands areas and does not rotate, the rotation turns without changing areas, and the shear is a combination of a stretching (along the diagonal) and a clockwise rotation, which preserves areas.}
    \label{fig:vector-fields}
\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:curl-grad}
    Let $f$ and $F$ be twice continuously differentiable.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The Jacobian of $\nabla f$ is the symmetric matrix $\nabla^2 f$. In particular, $\curl\nabla f = 0$ for $n = 2$ and $n = 3$.
        \item For $n = 3$, $\Div\curl F = 0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    (1) This is Schwarz's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:schwarz}): the entries of $\curl\nabla f$ are differences $\frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_i\partial x_j} - \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x_j\partial x_i} = 0$. (2) By definition,
    \[\Div\curl F = \frac{\partial^2 F_3}{\partial x_1\partial x_2} - \frac{\partial^2 F_2}{\partial x_1\partial x_3} + \frac{\partial^2 F_1}{\partial x_2\partial x_3} - \frac{\partial^2 F_3}{\partial x_2\partial x_1} + \frac{\partial^2 F_2}{\partial x_3\partial x_1} - \frac{\partial^2 F_1}{\partial x_3\partial x_2},\]
    and the terms cancel in pairs by Schwarz's theorem.
\end{proof}

So a gradient field has no rotational part. Conversely, a vector field on $\mathbb{R}^n$ whose Jacobian is symmetric everywhere is a gradient field (Theorem~\ref{thm:poincare} below), while a field with a nonzero curl is not the gradient of any function. This matters whenever we follow a vector field and hope to minimize something.

\begin{remark}[Vector fields in machine learning]\label{rem:vector-fields-ml}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item (Games.) Generative adversarial networks (GANs) train two networks against each other: a generator with the parameters $\mathbf{x}$ minimizes a function $f(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y})$, which a discriminator with the parameters $\mathbf{y}$ maximizes. Simultaneous gradient descent--ascent follows the vector field $F = (-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f, \nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f)$, which is in general not a gradient field. For $f(x, y) = xy$, $F(x, y) = (-y, x)$ is the rotation field, which is not a gradient field, since its curl is $2$. Gradient descent--ascent with the step size $\eta$ moves $(x, y)$ to $(x - \eta y, \, y + \eta x)$, which multiplies the distance from the equilibrium $(0, 0)$ by $\sqrt{1 + \eta^2}$: the iterates spiral outwards instead of converging (Example~\ref{ex:flow-volumes} studies the continuous-time version). The oscillations of GAN training come from such rotational parts of the dynamics, and methods such as extragradient are designed to damp them.
        \item (Scores.) A neural network $\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ that approximates a score $\nabla\log p$ (Example~\ref{ex:vector-fields}) need not have a symmetric Jacobian, so in general it is not exactly a gradient field. Energy-based models instead learn a scalar function $E_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ and use the gradient field $-\nabla E_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$.
        \item (Computing divergences.) For a vector field $F: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ given by a neural network, computing $\Div F = \tr DF$ exactly needs $n$ passes of automatic differentiation, one for each diagonal entry. Hutchinson's estimator uses that $\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top A\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}] = \tr(A\,\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top]) = \tr A$ for every random vector $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}$ with $\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top] = I$, for example with independent random signs as entries (expected values are treated in Lecture~4; see also Exercise~\ref{ex3:hutchinson}). So $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top DF(\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}$ is on average equal to $\Div F(\mathbf{x})$, and $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top DF(\mathbf{x})$ is a single vector-Jacobian product, computed by one pass of reverse mode (Section~\ref{sec:autodiff}). Averages over a few random vectors are used in score matching (Section~\ref{sec:integrals}) and in continuous normalizing flows (Section~\ref{sec:flows}).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Multiple Integrals and the Divergence Theorem}\label{sec:integrals}

Probabilities and expectations of random vectors are integrals of densities over regions of $\mathbb{R}^n$ (Lecture~4), and we have already used integrals over the plane for the change of variables in Section~\ref{sec:jacobians}. In this section, we define integrals of functions of several variables, compute them as iterated one-dimensional integrals, and prove the analogue of the fundamental theorem of calculus for vector fields, the divergence theorem. Its main consequence for us is a rule for integration by parts in $\mathbb{R}^n$, which is behind score matching and, in Section~\ref{sec:flows}, behind the evolution of probability densities under a flow.

\subsection{Integrals over boxes and Fubini's theorem}

A box is a product of intervals $Q = [a_1, b_1] \times \cdots \times [a_n, b_n]$, with the volume $\vol(Q) = (b_1 - a_1)\cdots(b_n - a_n)$. Dividing each interval into $k$ intervals of equal length divides $Q$ into $k^n$ small boxes $Q_1, \ldots, Q_{k^n}$, each with the volume $\frac{\vol(Q)}{k^n}$.

\begin{definition}[Integral over a box]\label{def:multiple-integral}
    A bounded function $f: Q \to \mathbb{R}$ is integrable if the Riemann sums $\sum_{j=1}^{k^n} f(\boldsymbol{\xi}_j)\vol(Q_j)$ converge as $k \to \infty$, to the same limit for all choices of points $\boldsymbol{\xi}_j \in Q_j$. The limit is the integral $\int_Q f(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x}$, also written $\iint_Q f(x, y)\, dx\, dy$ for $n = 2$.
\end{definition}

As in one variable, continuous functions on boxes are integrable, the integral is linear, and $f \leq g$ implies $\int_Q f \leq \int_Q g$. For $f \geq 0$, the integral is the volume of the region below the graph of $f$. To integrate over a bounded set $D$, we choose a box $Q \supseteq D$ and integrate the function that agrees with $f$ on $D$ and is $0$ outside, $\int_D f(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x} = \int_Q f(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{1}_D(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x}$, where $\mathbf{1}_D$ is the indicator function of $D$. This works, for example, for continuous functions $f$ and regions $D$ bounded by graphs of continuous functions. In particular, $\vol(D) = \int_D 1\, d\mathbf{x}$. Integrals over unbounded sets, such as $\mathbb{R}^n$, are defined as limits of integrals over growing boxes, like improper integrals in one variable.

Integrals of several variables are computed one variable at a time.

\begin{theorem}[Fubini]\label{thm:fubini}
    Let $f$ be continuous on the rectangle $Q = [a, b] \times [c, d]$. Then
    \[\iint_Q f(x, y)\, dx\, dy = \int_a^b \bigg(\int_c^d f(x, y)\, dy\bigg) dx = \int_c^d \bigg(\int_a^b f(x, y)\, dx\bigg) dy.\]
    The same holds for boxes in $\mathbb{R}^n$, integrating over one variable after the other in any order.
\end{theorem}

We do not give a complete proof, but the idea is simple. With the grid points $x_i$ and $y_j$, a Riemann sum is a double sum $\sum_i\sum_j f(x_i, y_j)\,\Delta x\,\Delta y$. Summing over $j$ first gives
\[\sum_i \Big(\sum_j f(x_i, y_j)\,\Delta y\Big)\Delta x \approx \sum_i \bigg(\int_c^d f(x_i, y)\, dy\bigg)\Delta x,\]
a Riemann sum of the iterated integral with $y$ first; summing over $i$ first gives the other order. Geometrically, we compute a volume by cutting it into slices and integrating the areas of the slices. For a region $D = \{(x, y) : a \leq x \leq b, \ g_1(x) \leq y \leq g_2(x)\}$ between the graphs of continuous functions $g_1 \leq g_2$, Fubini's theorem gives
\[\iint_D f(x, y)\, dx\, dy = \int_a^b \bigg(\int_{g_1(x)}^{g_2(x)} f(x, y)\, dy\bigg) dx.\]

\begin{example}\label{ex:fubini}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item For the triangle $T = \{(x, y) : 0 \leq y \leq x \leq 1\}$,
        \[\iint_T xy\, dx\, dy = \int_0^1 \int_0^x xy\, dy\, dx = \int_0^1 \frac{x^3}{2}\, dx = \frac{1}{8}.\]
        Describing $T$ by $0 \leq y \leq 1$ and $y \leq x \leq 1$ gives the same result, $\int_0^1 \int_y^1 xy\, dx\, dy = \int_0^1 \frac{y(1 - y^2)}{2}\, dy = \frac{1}{8}$.
        \item (A probability.) Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent waiting times with the exponential densities $\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ and $\mu e^{-\mu y}$ on $[0, \infty)$, so that $(X, Y)$ has the joint density $\lambda\mu e^{-\lambda x - \mu y}$ (Lecture~4). The probability that $Y$ ends first is the integral of the joint density over $\{0 \leq y < x\}$:
        \begin{align*}
            P(Y < X) &= \int_0^\infty \lambda e^{-\lambda x}\bigg(\int_0^x \mu e^{-\mu y}\, dy\bigg) dx = \int_0^\infty \lambda e^{-\lambda x}(1 - e^{-\mu x})\, dx \\
            &= 1 - \frac{\lambda}{\lambda + \mu} = \frac{\mu}{\lambda + \mu}.
        \end{align*}
        \item (The order can matter.) Let $f(x, y) = \frac{x^2 - y^2}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$ on $(0, 1]^2$. Since $\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\frac{y}{x^2 + y^2} = f(x, y)$, we get $\int_0^1 f(x, y)\, dy = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$ and $\int_0^1\int_0^1 f(x, y)\, dy\, dx = \frac{\pi}{4}$. Since $f(y, x) = -f(x, y)$, the other order gives $-\frac{\pi}{4}$. Fubini's theorem does not apply, because $f$ is unbounded near the origin; in fact, $\iint |f| = \infty$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

For unbounded functions or regions, Fubini's theorem remains true if $f \geq 0$ or if $\int |f| < \infty$ (the Fubini--Tonelli theorem in the appendix to Lecture~4), which covers the integrals of probability theory.

\subsection{Change of variables}

\begin{theorem}[Change of variables]\label{thm:change-of-variables}
    Let $\Phi: U \to V$ be a bijection between open sets $U, V \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$, such that $\Phi$ and $\Phi^{-1}$ are continuously differentiable. Then for every integrable function $g$ on $V$,
    \[\int_V g(\mathbf{y})\, d\mathbf{y} = \int_U g(\Phi(\mathbf{x}))\,|\det D\Phi(\mathbf{x})|\, d\mathbf{x}.\]
\end{theorem}

This is the formula of Remark~\ref{rem:change-of-variables}, and it comes from the interpretation of $|\det D\Phi(\mathbf{x})|$ as the factor by which $\Phi$ scales small volumes (Section~\ref{sec:jacobians}): the images $\Phi(Q_j)$ of small boxes $Q_j$ in $U$ cover $V$ and have the volumes $|\det D\Phi(\boldsymbol{\xi}_j)|\vol(Q_j)$ approximately, so Riemann sums for the two sides are approximately equal. We do not make this precise.

\begin{example}[The Gaussian integral in $n$ dimensions]\label{ex:gauss-integral}
    Let $\Sigma$ be symmetric positive definite. We show that
    \[\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} \exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{x}\big)\, d\mathbf{x} = (2\pi)^{n/2}\sqrt{\det\Sigma},\]
    which gives the normalizing constant of the multivariate Gaussian distribution in Lecture~4. For $\Sigma = I$, the integrand is the product $e^{-x_1^2/2}\cdots e^{-x_n^2/2}$, so by Fubini's theorem the integral is the product of $n$ one-dimensional integrals $\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-t^2/2}\, dt = \sqrt{2\pi}$ (Example~\ref{ex:polar}, with the substitution $t = \sqrt{2}x$). In general, write $\Sigma = AA^\top$ with an invertible matrix $A$, for example $A = Q\Lambda^{1/2}$ from the spectral decomposition $\Sigma = Q\Lambda Q^\top$ (Lecture~2), where $\Lambda^{1/2}$ is the diagonal matrix of the square roots of the positive eigenvalues. Substitute $\mathbf{x} = A\mathbf{z}$. Then $\mathbf{x}^\top\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{z}^\top A^\top A^{-\top}A^{-1}A\mathbf{z} = \norm{\mathbf{z}}^2$ and $|\det A| = \sqrt{\det\Sigma}$, so the integral is $\sqrt{\det\Sigma}\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} e^{-\norm{\mathbf{z}}^2/2}\, d\mathbf{z} = (2\pi)^{n/2}\sqrt{\det\Sigma}$.
\end{example}

\subsection{Differentiating under the integral sign}

\begin{proposition}[Leibniz rule]\label{prop:leibniz}
    Let $f(x, \theta)$ and $\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}(x, \theta)$ be continuous for $x \in [a, b]$ and $\theta$ in an open interval $I$. Then
    \[\frac{d}{d\theta}\int_a^b f(x, \theta)\, dx = \int_a^b \frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}(x, \theta)\, dx \qquad \text{for all } \theta \in I.\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    The function $g(\theta) = \int_a^b \frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}(x, \theta)\, dx$ is continuous, since $\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}$ is uniformly continuous on closed and bounded sets (see the separate appendix on topology, differential topology and functional analysis). Fix $\theta_0 \in I$. By Fubini's theorem and the fundamental theorem of calculus,
    \[\int_{\theta_0}^\theta g(s)\, ds = \int_a^b \int_{\theta_0}^\theta \frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}(x, s)\, ds\, dx = \int_a^b f(x, \theta)\, dx - \int_a^b f(x, \theta_0)\, dx.\]
    Differentiating with respect to $\theta$ gives the claim, again by the fundamental theorem of calculus.
\end{proof}

The same holds for integrals over boxes in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and for several parameters, and, under an additional condition (for example, if $|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta}|$ is bounded by an integrable function that does not depend on $\theta$), for integrals over unbounded regions such as $\mathbb{R}^n$ (the appendix to Lecture~4 proves this with the dominated convergence theorem). In machine learning, we often need the gradient of an expectation $\int g(\mathbf{x})p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x}$ with respect to the parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ of a distribution, for example of a policy in reinforcement learning. Differentiating under the integral sign and using $\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}} = p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ gives
\[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\int g(\mathbf{x})p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x} = \int g(\mathbf{x})\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\log p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\,p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x},\]
again an expectation with respect to $p_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$, which can be estimated by averaging over samples. This is the score function (or REINFORCE) estimator of policy gradient methods.

\subsection{Line integrals and gradient fields}

\begin{definition}[Line integral]\label{def:line-integral}
    Let $\gamma: [a, b] \to D$ be a continuously differentiable curve, and let $F$ be a continuous vector field on $D$. The line integral of $F$ along $\gamma$ is
    \[\int_\gamma F \cdot d\mathbf{x} = \int_a^b F(\gamma(t))^\top\gamma'(t)\, dt.\]
\end{definition}

If $F$ is a force, the line integral is the work done by it along the path. By the substitution rule, the line integral does not change if we reparametrize the curve in the same direction, and it changes its sign if we reverse the direction. For gradient fields, it only depends on the endpoints.

\begin{theorem}[Fundamental theorem for line integrals]\label{thm:gradient-theorem}
    If $f$ is continuously differentiable on $D$, then for every continuously differentiable curve $\gamma: [a, b] \to D$,
    \[\int_\gamma \nabla f \cdot d\mathbf{x} = f(\gamma(b)) - f(\gamma(a)).\]
    In particular, the line integral of a gradient field along a closed curve ($\gamma(a) = \gamma(b)$) is $0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    By the chain rule (Corollary~\ref{cor:chain-special}), $\frac{d}{dt}f(\gamma(t)) = \nabla f(\gamma(t))^\top\gamma'(t)$; now integrate from $a$ to $b$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Integrated gradients]\label{ex:integrated-gradients}
    To explain the prediction $f(\mathbf{x})$ of a model, the method of integrated gradients compares $\mathbf{x}$ with a baseline input $\mathbf{x}'$, such as a black image, and assigns to the $i$-th input feature the attribution
    \[A_i = (x_i - x_i')\int_0^1 \frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}\big(\mathbf{x}' + t(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}')\big)\, dt.\]
    The sum of the attributions is the line integral of $\nabla f$ along the segment $\gamma(t) = \mathbf{x}' + t(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}')$, so $A_1 + \cdots + A_n = f(\mathbf{x}) - f(\mathbf{x}')$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:gradient-theorem}: the attributions account exactly for the change of the output. For example, for $f(\mathbf{x}) = x_1x_2$, $\mathbf{x} = (2, 3)^\top$ and $\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{0}$, we get $A_1 = 2\int_0^1 3t\, dt = 3$ and $A_2 = 3\int_0^1 2t\, dt = 3$, which add up to $f(\mathbf{x}) = 6$. The simpler attributions $x_i\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}(\mathbf{x})$ (``gradient times input'') are $6$ and $6$, which add up to $12$.
\end{example}

Conversely, if the line integrals of $F$ only depend on the endpoints, then $F$ is a gradient field: fix a point $\mathbf{x}_0$, and let $f(\mathbf{x})$ be the line integral of $F$ along any curve from $\mathbf{x}_0$ to $\mathbf{x}$. On $\mathbb{R}^n$, a symmetric Jacobian is enough.

\begin{theorem}[Poincar\'e lemma]\label{thm:poincare}
    Let $F: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ be continuously differentiable, with a symmetric Jacobian $DF(\mathbf{x})$ for every $\mathbf{x}$. Then $F = \nabla f$ for the function
    \[f(\mathbf{x}) = \int_0^1 F(t\mathbf{x})^\top\mathbf{x}\, dt,\]
    the line integral of $F$ along the segment from $\mathbf{0}$ to $\mathbf{x}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    By the Leibniz rule and the product rule, and since $\frac{\partial F_j}{\partial x_i} = \frac{\partial F_i}{\partial x_j}$,
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}(\mathbf{x}) &= \int_0^1 \Big(F_i(t\mathbf{x}) + \sum_{j=1}^n t\,\frac{\partial F_j}{\partial x_i}(t\mathbf{x})\,x_j\Big)\, dt = \int_0^1 \Big(F_i(t\mathbf{x}) + t\sum_{j=1}^n \frac{\partial F_i}{\partial x_j}(t\mathbf{x})\,x_j\Big)\, dt.
    \end{align*}
    By the chain rule, the integrand is $\frac{d}{dt}\big(tF_i(t\mathbf{x})\big)$, so $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}(\mathbf{x}) = 1 \cdot F_i(\mathbf{x}) - 0 = F_i(\mathbf{x})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A field with zero curl that is not a gradient field]\label{ex:vortex}
    On $D = \mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \{\mathbf{0}\}$, let $F(x, y) = \frac{1}{x^2 + y^2}(-y, x)^\top$. A short computation gives $\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x} = \frac{y^2 - x^2}{(x^2 + y^2)^2} = \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial y}$, so $\curl F = 0$. But along the unit circle $\gamma(t) = (\cos t, \sin t)^\top$, $0 \leq t \leq 2\pi$, we have $F(\gamma(t)) = (-\sin t, \cos t)^\top = \gamma'(t)$, so
    \[\int_\gamma F \cdot d\mathbf{x} = \int_0^{2\pi} (\sin^2 t + \cos^2 t)\, dt = 2\pi \neq 0,\]
    and $F$ is not a gradient field on $D$. Near each point, $F$ is the gradient of the polar angle, but the angle cannot be defined continuously along a closed curve around the origin. The Poincar\'e lemma does not apply because $D$ has a hole: whether vector fields with zero curl are gradient fields depends on the topology of the domain.
\end{example}

\subsection{The divergence theorem}

Let $V \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$ be a bounded region whose boundary $\partial V$ is smooth, such as a ball, or a box (whose boundary is smooth except at the edges), and let $\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x})$ be the outward unit normal vector at $\mathbf{x} \in \partial V$. We also need integrals $\int_{\partial V} g\, dS$ over the boundary, with respect to its $(n-1)$-dimensional volume (length for $n = 2$, area for $n = 3$). We do not define them in general. For $n = 2$ and a curve $\gamma: [a, b] \to \mathbb{R}^2$ that traverses $\partial V$ once, $\int_{\partial V} g\, dS = \int_a^b g(\gamma(t))\norm{\gamma'(t)}\, dt$; and the faces of a box are boxes of dimension $n - 1$.

\begin{theorem}[Divergence theorem]\label{thm:divergence}
    For every continuously differentiable vector field $F$ on an open set that contains $V$ and $\partial V$,
    \[\int_V \Div F(\mathbf{x})\, d\mathbf{x} = \int_{\partial V} F^\top\mathbf{n}\, dS.\]
\end{theorem}

The right side is the flux of $F$ through the boundary: if $F$ is the velocity field of a fluid, it is the net amount of fluid that flows out of $V$ per unit time. So the theorem says that the total strength of the sources inside $V$ equals the net outflow through its boundary, which shows again that the divergence is the outflow per unit volume. For $n = 1$ and $V = [a, b]$, with the outward normals $+1$ at $b$ and $-1$ at $a$, it is the fundamental theorem of calculus $\int_a^b F'(x)\, dx = F(b) - F(a)$.

\begin{proof}[Proof for a box]
    Let $V = [a_1, b_1] \times \cdots \times [a_n, b_n]$, and consider the term $\frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_1}$ of $\Div F$. Write $\mathbf{x} = (x_1, \tilde{\mathbf{x}})$ with $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ in the box $\tilde Q = [a_2, b_2] \times \cdots \times [a_n, b_n]$. By Fubini's theorem and the fundamental theorem of calculus,
    \begin{align*}
        \int_V \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_1}\, d\mathbf{x} &= \int_{\tilde Q}\bigg(\int_{a_1}^{b_1}\frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_1}(x_1, \tilde{\mathbf{x}})\, dx_1\bigg) d\tilde{\mathbf{x}} = \int_{\tilde Q} F_1(b_1, \tilde{\mathbf{x}})\, d\tilde{\mathbf{x}} - \int_{\tilde Q} F_1(a_1, \tilde{\mathbf{x}})\, d\tilde{\mathbf{x}}.
    \end{align*}
    This is the flux of the vector field $F_1\mathbf{e}_1$ through the boundary of $V$: through the face $x_1 = b_1$ with the outward normal $\mathbf{e}_1$, through the face $x_1 = a_1$ with the outward normal $-\mathbf{e}_1$, and nothing through the other faces, whose normals $\pm\mathbf{e}_j$ ($j \neq 1$) are orthogonal to $\mathbf{e}_1$. Adding the same identities for $F_2\mathbf{e}_2, \ldots, F_n\mathbf{e}_n$ proves the theorem for $V$.
\end{proof}

For a general region, one approximates $V$ by many small boxes. When we add the identities for all boxes, the fluxes through the faces shared by two neighboring boxes cancel, since the outward normal of one box is the inward normal of the other, and only the fluxes through the outer boundary remain. Making this precise requires more care, and we omit it.

\begin{example}\label{ex:divergence-theorem}
    For $F(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}$, $\Div F = n$, and on the sphere of radius $R$, $\mathbf{n} = \frac{\mathbf{x}}{R}$ and $F^\top\mathbf{n} = R$. So the divergence theorem for the ball $B_R$ of radius $R$ says that $n\vol(B_R) = R\area(\partial B_R)$: for $n = 2$, $2\pi R^2 = R \cdot 2\pi R$, and for $n = 3$, $3 \cdot \frac{4}{3}\pi R^3 = R \cdot 4\pi R^2$.
\end{example}

In the plane, the divergence theorem has a second form. If $\gamma$ traverses $\partial V$ counterclockwise (with $V$ on its left), then the unit tangent vector is $\boldsymbol{\tau} = \frac{\gamma'}{\norm{\gamma'}}$, and the outward normal is $\boldsymbol{\tau}$ rotated clockwise by $90^\circ$, $\mathbf{n} = (\tau_2, -\tau_1)^\top$. For the vector field $G = (F_2, -F_1)^\top$, we have $\Div G = \curl F$ and $G^\top\mathbf{n} = F^\top\boldsymbol{\tau}$, so the divergence theorem for $G$ gives the following.

\begin{theorem}[Green]\label{thm:green}
    Let $V \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2$ be a bounded region whose boundary is a closed curve $\gamma$, traversed counterclockwise. Then for every continuously differentiable vector field $F$ on an open set that contains $V$ and $\partial V$,
    \[\int_\gamma F \cdot d\mathbf{x} = \iint_V \curl F\, dx\, dy.\]
\end{theorem}

The left side is the circulation of $F$ around $V$, so the curl is the circulation per unit area. For example, $F = \frac{1}{2}(-y, x)^\top$ has $\curl F = 1$, so the area of $V$ is the line integral of $F$ along its boundary. (The vortex field of Example~\ref{ex:vortex} is not differentiable at the origin, so Green's theorem does not apply to it on the unit disk.)

\begin{remark}[Stokes' theorem]
    In three dimensions, Stokes' theorem says that $\int_{\partial S} F \cdot d\mathbf{x} = \int_S (\curl F)^\top\mathbf{n}\, dS$ for a surface $S$ with the boundary curve $\partial S$; for a flat surface, it is Green's theorem. The fundamental theorem of calculus, the fundamental theorem for line integrals, Green's theorem, the divergence theorem and Stokes' theorem are all special cases of one theorem about differential forms on manifolds, the general Stokes theorem: the integral of a derivative over a region equals an integral over its boundary. Differential forms and this general Stokes theorem are treated in the separate appendix on topology, differential topology and functional analysis. For machine learning, the most important of these theorems is the divergence theorem, through its consequence, integration by parts.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Integration by parts and its applications}

\begin{corollary}[Integration by parts]\label{cor:integration-by-parts}
    Let $g: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ and $F: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ be continuously differentiable. Then
    \[\int_V g\Div F\, d\mathbf{x} = \int_{\partial V} gF^\top\mathbf{n}\, dS - \int_V \nabla g^\top F\, d\mathbf{x}.\]
    If $gF$ vanishes outside a bounded set, or decays so fast that its flux through spheres of radius $R$ tends to $0$ as $R \to \infty$, then $\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} g\Div F\, d\mathbf{x} = -\int_{\mathbb{R}^n} \nabla g^\top F\, d\mathbf{x}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    By the product rule, $\frac{\partial}{\partial x_i}(gF_i) = \frac{\partial g}{\partial x_i}F_i + g\frac{\partial F_i}{\partial x_i}$, and summing over $i$ gives $\Div(gF) = \nabla g^\top F + g\Div F$. Now apply the divergence theorem to $gF$. For the second statement, take for $V$ balls with growing radii.
\end{proof}

For $n = 1$, this is the familiar rule $\int_a^b gF'\, dx = [gF]_a^b - \int_a^b g'F\, dx$. We give an application to machine learning; in Section~\ref{sec:flows}, the same rule describes how probability densities move under the flow of a differential equation.

\begin{example}[Score matching]\label{ex:score-matching}
    We want to fit a model $\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ of the score $\nabla\log p$ of an unknown data density $p$, given samples from $p$ (Example~\ref{ex:vector-fields}). The natural loss
    \[J(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \frac{1}{2}\int p(\mathbf{x})\norm{\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}) - \nabla\log p(\mathbf{x})}^2\, d\mathbf{x}\]
    seems useless, since it contains the unknown score. Expanding the square gives $\frac{1}{2}\int p\norm{\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^2\, d\mathbf{x} - \int p\,\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^\top\nabla\log p\, d\mathbf{x} + C$, where $C$ does not depend on $\boldsymbol{\theta}$. Since $p\nabla\log p = \nabla p$, integration by parts (with $g = p$ and $F = \mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$, assuming that $p\,\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ decays fast enough) turns the middle term into
    \[-\int \mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^\top\nabla p\, d\mathbf{x} = \int p\Div\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\, d\mathbf{x}.\]
    So $J(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = \int p(\mathbf{x})\big(\frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})}^2 + \Div\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\big)\, d\mathbf{x} + C$: an average over the data distribution, in which the unknown score no longer appears. It is estimated by the average of $\frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_i)}^2 + \Div\mathbf{s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\mathbf{x}_i)$ over the training data $\mathbf{x}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{x}_N$, with Hutchinson's estimator for the divergence (Remark~\ref{rem:vector-fields-ml}). This is Hyv\"arinen's score matching; diffusion models use a variant of it, denoising score matching.
\end{example}

\section{Ordinary Differential Equations}\label{sec:ode}

A differential equation describes how a state changes continuously in time: at every moment, the rate of change of the state is a given function of the state. This function is a vector field, and the state moves along its arrows, with the velocity given by the arrow at its current position. Differential equations appear in machine learning in the analysis of training dynamics, in neural networks with continuous depth, and, with randomness added, in diffusion models and sampling algorithms.

\begin{definition}[Ordinary differential equation]\label{def:ode}
    Let $F: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ be a vector field (Definition~\ref{def:vector-field}). A solution of the ordinary differential equation (ODE) $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ with the initial value $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}_0$ is a differentiable curve $\mathbf{x}(t)$ with $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}_0$ and $\mathbf{x}'(t) = F(\mathbf{x}(t))$ for all $t$.
\end{definition}

Geometrically, a solution is a curve that is tangent to the vector field at each of its points: at the point $\mathbf{x}$, it moves with the velocity $F(\mathbf{x})$.

\begin{theorem}[Existence and uniqueness]\label{thm:picard}
    If $F$ is Lipschitz continuous, i.e., $\norm{F(\mathbf{x}) - F(\mathbf{y})} \leq L\norm{\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{y}}$ for all $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}$, then for every $\mathbf{x}_0$, the ODE $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ has exactly one solution with $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}_0$, defined for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$.
\end{theorem}

This is the Picard--Lindel\"of theorem. The idea of the proof is that $\mathbf{x}$ solves the problem if and only if $\mathbf{x}(t) = \mathbf{x}_0 + \int_0^t F(\mathbf{x}(s))\, ds$ for all $t$, so a solution is a fixed point of the map that sends a curve $\mathbf{x}$ to the right-hand side; with respect to a suitable norm, this map is a contraction, and the Banach fixed-point theorem applies. The separate appendix carries out this proof.

\begin{example}\label{ex:scalar-ode}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The solution of $x' = ax$ with $x(0) = x_0$ is $x(t) = e^{at}x_0$, since $\frac{d}{dt}e^{at}x_0 = ae^{at}x_0$. It grows exponentially if $a > 0$ and decays to $0$ if $a < 0$.
        \item The logistic equation $x' = x(1 - x)$ models growth with a limited capacity. By Example~\ref{ex:sigmoid}, the sigmoid function satisfies $\sigma' = \sigma(1 - \sigma)$, so $x(t) = \sigma(t + c)$ is a solution, with $c$ chosen such that $\sigma(c) = x_0$. For $0 < x_0 < 1$, the solution increases and tends to $1$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\subsection{Linear systems}\label{sec:linear-ode}

For a matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, the linear ODE $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ couples the coordinates, and eigenvectors decouple them (Lecture~1).

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:linear-ode}
