    Let $A = V\Lambda V^{-1}$ be diagonalizable, with eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n$ and eigenvectors $\mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_n$ (the columns of $V$), and write $\mathbf{x}_0 = c_1\mathbf{v}_1 + \cdots + c_n\mathbf{v}_n$. Then the solution of $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ with $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}_0$ is
    \[\mathbf{x}(t) = c_1e^{\lambda_1 t}\mathbf{v}_1 + \cdots + c_ne^{\lambda_n t}\mathbf{v}_n.\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Each term satisfies $\frac{d}{dt}c_ie^{\lambda_i t}\mathbf{v}_i = \lambda_ic_ie^{\lambda_i t}\mathbf{v}_i = A(c_ie^{\lambda_i t}\mathbf{v}_i)$, so the sum is a solution, and it has the right initial value. It is the only one by Theorem~\ref{thm:picard}, since $\norm{A\mathbf{x} - A\mathbf{y}} \leq \norm{A}_2\norm{\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{y}}$. (Equivalently, the coordinates $\mathbf{y} = V^{-1}\mathbf{x}$ satisfy the decoupled equations $y_i' = \lambda_i y_i$.)
\end{proof}

More generally, the solution is $\mathbf{x}(t) = e^{At}\mathbf{x}_0$ with the matrix exponential $e^{At} = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{(At)^k}{k!}$, which converges for every matrix and satisfies $\frac{d}{dt}e^{At} = Ae^{At}$; for diagonalizable $A$, $e^{At} = Ve^{\Lambda t}V^{-1}$ with $e^{\Lambda t} = \diag(e^{\lambda_1 t}, \ldots, e^{\lambda_n t})$. So the eigenvalues determine the long-term behavior: if all (complex) eigenvalues have negative real parts, every solution tends to $\mathbf{0}$, and if some eigenvalue has a positive real part, solutions starting in the direction of its eigenvector grow exponentially. This is the continuous-time analogue of the powers $P^t$ of a matrix, which govern gradient descent (Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-quadratic}) and Markov chains (Lecture~4).

\begin{example}\label{ex:linear-ode}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Let $A = \begin{pmatrix} -2 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$, with the eigenvalues $-1$ and $-3$ and the eigenvectors $\mathbf{v}_1 = (1, 1)^\top$ and $\mathbf{v}_2 = (1, -1)^\top$. For $\mathbf{x}_0 = (2, 0)^\top = \mathbf{v}_1 + \mathbf{v}_2$, the solution is $\mathbf{x}(t) = e^{-t}(1, 1)^\top + e^{-3t}(1, -1)^\top$; for example, $\mathbf{x}(1) \approx (0.418, 0.318)^\top$. The component along $\mathbf{v}_2$ decays faster, so all solutions approach the origin along the slow direction $\mathbf{v}_1$ (Figure~\ref{fig:ode}).
        \item Let $A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$, with the complex eigenvalues $\pm i$. Then $e^{At} = \begin{pmatrix} \cos t & -\sin t \\ \sin t & \cos t \end{pmatrix}$ is the rotation by the angle $t$ (Lecture~2): one checks that this matrix is $I$ at $t = 0$ and that its derivative is $A$ times itself. The solutions move on circles. Indeed, for every skew-symmetric $A$, $\frac{d}{dt}\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2 = 2\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x} = 0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.15, >=Stealth]
        \draw[dashed, gray] (-2.3,-2.3) -- (2.3,2.3) node[above right, black] {$\mathbf{v}_1$};
        \draw[dashed, gray] (-2.3,2.3) -- (2.3,-2.3) node[below right, black] {$\mathbf{v}_2$};
        \foreach \x in {-2,-1.5,...,2}
            \foreach \y in {-2,-1.5,...,2}
                \draw[->, gray!70] (\x,\y) -- ++({0.09*(-2*\x + \y)},{0.09*(\x - 2*\y)});
        \foreach \a in {15,45,...,345}
            \draw[->, thick, blue!70!black] plot[domain=0:4, samples=60, variable=\t]
                ({(cos(\a) + sin(\a))*exp(-\t) + (cos(\a) - sin(\a))*exp(-3*\t)}, {(cos(\a) + sin(\a))*exp(-\t) - (cos(\a) - sin(\a))*exp(-3*\t)});
        \draw[->, gray] (-2.5,0) -- (2.6,0) node[right] {$x_1$};
        \draw[->, gray] (0,-2.5) -- (0,2.6) node[above] {$x_2$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
    \caption{The vector field $A\mathbf{x}$ (gray) and solutions of $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ (blue) for $A = \left(\begin{smallmatrix} -2 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{smallmatrix}\right)$, starting on the circle of radius $2$. The component along the eigenvector $\mathbf{v}_2$ (eigenvalue $-3$) dies out quickly, and the solutions approach the origin along $\mathbf{v}_1$ (eigenvalue $-1$).}
    \label{fig:ode}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Gradient flow and Euler's method}\label{sec:euler}

\begin{definition}[Gradient flow]\label{def:gradient-flow}
    The gradient flow of a differentiable function $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ is the ODE $\mathbf{x}' = -\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:gradient-flow}
    Along every solution of the gradient flow, $\frac{d}{dt}f(\mathbf{x}(t)) = -\norm{\nabla f(\mathbf{x}(t))}^2 \leq 0$: the value of $f$ decreases, and strictly unless $\mathbf{x}(t)$ is a critical point.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    By the chain rule (Corollary~\ref{cor:chain-special}), $\frac{d}{dt}f(\mathbf{x}(t)) = \nabla f(\mathbf{x}(t))^\top\mathbf{x}'(t) = -\norm{\nabla f(\mathbf{x}(t))}^2$.
\end{proof}

For the quadratic function $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b}^\top\mathbf{x}$ with symmetric positive definite $A$, the error $\mathbf{e} = \mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}^*$ satisfies $\mathbf{e}' = -A\mathbf{e}$, so by Proposition~\ref{prop:linear-ode}, $\mathbf{e}(t) = \sum_i c_ie^{-\lambda_i t}\mathbf{q}_i$ in an orthonormal eigenbasis of $A$. The gradient flow converges to the minimum for every positive definite $A$, and the slowest direction decays like $e^{-\lambda_{\min}t}$.

To solve an ODE $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ numerically, Euler's method follows the tangent for a short time $h$:
\[\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + hF(\mathbf{x}_k), \qquad \text{so that } \mathbf{x}_k \approx \mathbf{x}(kh).\]
For a smooth $F$, its error on a fixed time interval is proportional to $h$. For example, for $x' = -x$ with $x(0) = 1$, Euler's method gives $x_k = (1 - h)^k$, so it approximates $x(1) = e^{-1} \approx 0.368$ by $0.25$ for $h = 0.5$, and by $0.9^{10} \approx 0.349$ for $h = 0.1$.

For the gradient flow, Euler's method is $\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k - h\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)$: gradient descent is Euler's method for the gradient flow, with the step size $\eta = h$. This explains the results of Section~\ref{sec:gd}. For the quadratic function, the flow converges for every positive definite $A$, but Euler's method multiplies the error in the direction $\mathbf{q}_i$ by $1 - h\lambda_i$, which is stable only if $h < \frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$. The directions of large curvature force small steps, while the directions of small curvature need the time $\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}$, so about $\kappa = \frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}$ steps are needed. Such ODEs are called stiff, and the zig-zagging of Figure~\ref{fig:gd} is an instability of the discretization, not of the flow.

\begin{remark}[ODEs in machine learning]
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The layers of a residual network compute $\mathbf{x}_{l+1} = \mathbf{x}_l + f_l(\mathbf{x}_l)$, which is an Euler step of an ODE $\mathbf{x}' = f(\mathbf{x}, t)$ with $h = 1$. Neural ODEs replace the layers by an ODE solver, and compute gradients by solving an adjoint ODE backwards in time, which is reverse-mode differentiation (Section~\ref{sec:autodiff}) in continuous time.
        \item Gradient descent with momentum is a discretization of the second-order ODE $\mathbf{x}'' + \gamma\mathbf{x}' + \nabla f(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{0}$ of a heavy ball with friction rolling in the landscape $f$; with $\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{x}'$, it is a first-order system for $(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{v})$.
        \item Adding random noise to an ODE gives a stochastic differential equation. Langevin dynamics and diffusion models, which sample from probability distributions in this way, are discussed in Lecture~4.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\section{Flows, Volumes and Densities}\label{sec:flows}

Instead of following a single solution, we can move all points at once. Let $\varphi_t(\mathbf{x}_0)$ be the solution of $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ with $\mathbf{x}(0) = \mathbf{x}_0$ at the time $t$. The map $\varphi_t$, the flow of $F$, moves every point along the vector field for the time $t$; for example, the flow of $F(\mathbf{x}) = A\mathbf{x}$ is $\varphi_t(\mathbf{x}) = e^{At}\mathbf{x}$. In Section~\ref{sec:vector-fields}, we saw that the divergence $\Div F = \tr DF$ is the net outflow of $F$ per unit volume. For the flow, this means that the divergence is the rate at which the flow expands volumes. For linear vector fields, the effect of the flow on volumes can be computed exactly.

\begin{proposition}[Liouville's formula]\label{prop:liouville}
    For every diagonalizable $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$, $\det e^{At} = e^{t\tr A}$. So the flow $\varphi_t(\mathbf{x}) = e^{At}\mathbf{x}$ of $F(\mathbf{x}) = A\mathbf{x}$ multiplies all volumes by $e^{t\Div F}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    With $A = V\Lambda V^{-1}$, $\det e^{At} = \det(V)\det(e^{\Lambda t})\det(V)^{-1} = e^{\lambda_1 t}\cdots e^{\lambda_n t} = e^{t(\lambda_1 + \cdots + \lambda_n)} = e^{t\tr A}$, and the linear map $e^{At}$ multiplies volumes by $|\det e^{At}|$ (Lecture~1).
\end{proof}

(The formula holds for every square matrix; Exercise~\ref{ex3:nilpotent} checks it for a matrix that is not diagonalizable.) For a general vector field and a small $t$, the flow $\varphi_t(\mathbf{y}) \approx \mathbf{y} + tF(\mathbf{y})$ has approximately the Jacobian $I + tDF(\mathbf{y})$, whose determinant is $1 + t\Div F(\mathbf{y})$ up to terms of order $t^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:det-identity}). So a small region near $\mathbf{x}$ with the volume $v$ has, after the short time $t$, approximately the volume $(1 + t\Div F(\mathbf{x}))v$:
\begin{center}
    \emph{$\Div F(\mathbf{x})$ is the rate at which the flow of $F$ expands volumes near $\mathbf{x}$.}
\end{center}
In particular, the flow of a vector field with $\Div F = 0$ preserves volumes.

\begin{example}\label{ex:flow-volumes}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $F(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}$ has $\Div F = n$. Its flow $\varphi_t(\mathbf{x}) = e^t\mathbf{x}$ multiplies lengths by $e^t$ and volumes by $e^{nt}$.
        \item The rotation field $(-y, x)^\top$ has $\Div F = 0$, and its flow, the rotation by the angle $t$ (Example~\ref{ex:linear-ode}), preserves areas. It is the continuous-time version of gradient descent--ascent for the game $\min_x\max_y xy$ (Remark~\ref{rem:vector-fields-ml}): the flow circles around the equilibrium forever, and Euler's method, i.e., gradient descent--ascent with a constant step size, spirals outwards (Exercise~\ref{ex3:euler-skew}).
        \item For the field $A\mathbf{x}$ of Figure~\ref{fig:ode}, $\Div F = \tr A = -4$, so the flow shrinks areas by the factor $e^{-4t}$, in agreement with the eigenvalues $-1$ and $-3$.
        \item The gradient flow $F = -\nabla f$ has $\Div F = -\Delta f = -\tr\nabla^2 f$. Near a strict local minimum, where the Hessian is positive definite, the gradient flow compresses volumes, and nearby solutions are drawn together.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\subsection{Transport of densities}

If the initial value of an ODE is random, then so is the solution at every time $t$, and the divergence also tells us how its probability density changes.

\begin{proposition}[Continuity equation]\label{prop:continuity}
    Let the initial value of the ODE $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ be random, with the density $p_0$, and let $p_t$ be the density of $\mathbf{x}(t)$. Then, if all functions are smooth enough,
    \[\frac{\partial p_t}{\partial t} = -\Div(p_tF), \qquad \text{and along every solution,} \qquad \frac{d}{dt}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}(t)) = -\Div F(\mathbf{x}(t)).\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Sketch of proof]
    Probability is neither created nor destroyed; it moves with the velocity $F$. So for a fixed region $V$, the probability $\int_V p_t\, d\mathbf{x}$ of $V$ changes by the flux of $p_tF$ out of $V$, and by the divergence theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:divergence}),
    \[\frac{d}{dt}\int_V p_t\, d\mathbf{x} = -\int_{\partial V} p_tF^\top\mathbf{n}\, dS = -\int_V \Div(p_tF)\, d\mathbf{x}.\]
    Differentiating under the integral sign on the left, the integrals of $\frac{\partial p_t}{\partial t}$ and of $-\Div(p_tF)$ agree on every region $V$, so the continuous functions agree. For the second formula, the chain rule and $\Div(p_tF) = \nabla p_t^\top F + p_t\Div F$ give
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt}p_t(\mathbf{x}(t)) &= \frac{\partial p_t}{\partial t}(\mathbf{x}(t)) + \nabla p_t(\mathbf{x}(t))^\top F(\mathbf{x}(t)) = -p_t(\mathbf{x}(t))\Div F(\mathbf{x}(t)),
    \end{align*}
    and we divide by $p_t(\mathbf{x}(t))$.
\end{proof}

The second formula is the continuous-time version of the change of variables formula of Remark~\ref{rem:change-of-variables}: where the flow expands volumes ($\Div F > 0$), the density along the solution decreases, and conversely. Continuous normalizing flows, which are generative models, transform a Gaussian distribution by the flow of an ODE $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ whose vector field is a neural network, and compute densities by
\[\log p_T(\mathbf{x}(T)) = \log p_0(\mathbf{x}(0)) - \int_0^T \Div F(\mathbf{x}(t))\, dt,\]
which replaces the determinant of a Jacobian by the integral of a trace, estimated with Hutchinson's estimator (Remark~\ref{rem:vector-fields-ml}). If noise is added to the ODE, the continuity equation becomes the Fokker--Planck equation (Lecture~4), which explains why Langevin dynamics samples from a given distribution.

\section{Key Takeaways}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item A function $f: D \to \mathbb{R}^m$ on an open set $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$ is differentiable at $\mathbf{x}$ if it is approximated by a linear map near $\mathbf{x}$: $f(\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{h}) = f(\mathbf{x}) + Df(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{h} + o(\norm{\mathbf{h}})$. The Jacobian $Df(\mathbf{x})$ is the $m \times n$ matrix of partial derivatives $\frac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_j}$; its rows belong to the outputs, its columns to the inputs. Existence of the partial derivatives alone is not enough, but continuous partial derivatives imply differentiability.
    \item For $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$, the derivative is a row vector (a linear functional), and the gradient $\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) = Df(\mathbf{x})^\top$ is the vector that represents it with the dot product. The directional derivative is $\nabla f(\mathbf{x})^\top\mathbf{v}$; by Cauchy--Schwarz, the gradient points in the direction of steepest ascent, and it is orthogonal to the level sets.
    \item Chain rule: $D(f \circ g)(\mathbf{x}) = Df(g(\mathbf{x}))\,Dg(\mathbf{x})$, the product of the Jacobians. For a scalar function, gradients are pulled back with transposed Jacobians: $\nabla(f \circ g)(\mathbf{x}) = Dg(\mathbf{x})^\top\nabla f(g(\mathbf{x}))$. Writing the sizes under each factor is a good check.
    \item Important gradients: $\nabla(\mathbf{a}^\top\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{a}$, $\nabla(\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}) = (A + A^\top)\mathbf{x}$, $\nabla\norm{A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b}}^2 = 2A^\top(A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b})$; for logistic regression and softmax with cross-entropy, the gradient with respect to the scores is ``prediction minus target''. For a linear layer $\mathbf{y} = W\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{b}$, $\nabla_W L = \boldsymbol{\delta}\mathbf{x}^\top$ and $\nabla_{\mathbf{x}} L = W^\top\boldsymbol{\delta}$ with $\boldsymbol{\delta} = \nabla_{\mathbf{y}} L$.
    \item Gradients with respect to matrices have the shape of the matrix, and satisfy $f(X + H) \approx f(X) + \ip{\nabla f(X)}{H}$ with the Frobenius inner product. Examples: $\nabla\tr(AX) = A^\top$, $\nabla\norm{AX - B}_F^2 = 2A^\top(AX - B)$, $\nabla\log|\det X| = X^{-\top}$.
    \item The Jacobian determinant $|\det Df(\mathbf{x})|$ is the local factor by which $f$ scales areas and volumes; it appears in the change of variables formula, e.g., the factor $r$ in polar coordinates.
    \item Automatic differentiation applies the chain rule to the elementary operations of a program. Forward mode computes Jacobian-vector products; reverse mode computes vector-Jacobian products and gives the whole gradient of a scalar function at a small multiple of the cost of evaluating it. Backpropagation is reverse mode for neural networks: $\boldsymbol{\delta}_l = \sigma'(\mathbf{z}_l) \odot (W_{l+1}^\top\boldsymbol{\delta}_{l+1})$.
    \item The Hessian $\nabla^2 f(\mathbf{x})$ is the symmetric matrix of second partial derivatives. Second-order Taylor: $f(\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{h}) \approx f(\mathbf{x}) + \nabla f(\mathbf{x})^\top\mathbf{h} + \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{h}^\top\nabla^2 f(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{h}$.
    \item At a local minimum, $\nabla f = \mathbf{0}$. At a critical point, the eigenvectors of the Hessian are the principal directions, and its eigenvalues are the curvatures along them: in these coordinates, $f \approx f(\mathbf{x}^*) + \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \lambda_i y_i^2$. All eigenvalues positive: strict local minimum (a bowl); all negative: strict local maximum; both signs: saddle point; some zero: the test is inconclusive.
    \item Gradient descent $\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)$ decreases an $L$-smooth function for $\eta \leq \frac{1}{L}$ and finds approximate critical points. On quadratic functions, it converges for $\eta < \frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$, at best with the rate $\frac{\kappa - 1}{\kappa + 1}$, where $\kappa$ is the condition number: ill-conditioned problems zig-zag. Backtracking line search finds step sizes of order $\frac{1}{L}$ without knowing $L$. Newton's method $\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k - \nabla^2 f(\mathbf{x}_k)^{-1}\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k)$ converges quadratically near a minimum, but is too expensive for large models; Gauss--Newton approximates the Hessian of $\frac{1}{2}\norm{\mathbf{r}}^2$ by $J^\top J$, and Levenberg--Marquardt damps it to $J^\top J + \lambda I$. Hessian-vector products cost a few gradients. SGD uses cheap, noisy mini-batch gradients.
    \item A differentiable function is convex if and only if it lies above its tangent planes, and a twice differentiable one if and only if its Hessian is positive semidefinite everywhere. For convex functions, every critical point and every local minimum is a global minimum. Least squares, logistic regression and softmax regression have convex losses; neural networks do not.
    \item A $\mu$-strongly convex function lies above the quadratic functions $f(\mathbf{x}) + \nabla f(\mathbf{x})^\top(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}) + \frac{\mu}{2}\norm{\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{x}}^2$ (for $C^2$ functions: all Hessian eigenvalues are at least $\mu$). For a convex $L$-smooth function, gradient descent with $\eta = \frac{1}{L}$ reaches $f(\mathbf{x}_k) - f^* \leq \frac{L\norm{\mathbf{x}_0 - \mathbf{x}^*}^2}{2k}$; with strong convexity, the error decreases geometrically, like $(1 - \frac{\mu}{L})^k$.
    \item Subgradients replace gradients for nonsmooth convex functions, and $\mathbf{x}^*$ minimizes $f$ if and only if $\mathbf{0} \in \partial f(\mathbf{x}^*)$. The proximal gradient method $\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}(\mathbf{x}_k - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x}_k))$ minimizes $f + h$; for the lasso, the proximal step is soft thresholding, which sets small weights exactly to $0$.
    \item Lagrange multipliers: at a constrained local minimum (with linearly independent constraint gradients), $\nabla f + \sum_i \lambda_i\nabla g_i = \mathbf{0}$. This gives eigenvectors for $\max_{\norm{\mathbf{x}} = 1}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ and the minimum norm solution $A^\top(AA^\top)^{-1}\mathbf{b}$. For inequality constraints, the KKT conditions add $\mu_i \geq 0$ and complementary slackness $\mu_i g_i(\mathbf{x}^*) = 0$, and for convex problems they are sufficient.
    \item The dual function $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) = \inf_{\mathbf{x}} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}, \boldsymbol{\mu})$ is concave, and $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) \leq p^*$ for all $\boldsymbol{\mu} \geq \mathbf{0}$ (weak duality). For convex problems in which the KKT conditions hold, the KKT multipliers maximize $d$ and $d^* = p^*$ (strong duality). The dual of the support vector machine only depends on the inner products $\mathbf{x}_i^\top\mathbf{x}_j$, which leads to kernel methods. The convex conjugate $f^*(\mathbf{y}) = \sup_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y}^\top\mathbf{x} - f(\mathbf{x}))$ satisfies $f + f^* \geq \mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{y}$ and $f^{**} = f$ for convex differentiable $f$; for linear constraints $A\mathbf{x} \leq \mathbf{b}$, $d(\boldsymbol{\mu}) = -\mathbf{b}^\top\boldsymbol{\mu} - f^*(-A^\top\boldsymbol{\mu})$, so the dual of a linear program is a linear program, and the dual of a quadratic program has only sign constraints.
    \item A vector field $F: D \to \mathbb{R}^n$ has a square Jacobian, which splits into a symmetric part (stretching) and a skew-symmetric part (rotation). The divergence $\Div F = \tr DF$ is the net outflow of $F$ per unit volume, and the Laplacian is $\Delta f = \Div\nabla f = \tr\nabla^2 f$. The curl ($\frac{\partial F_2}{\partial x_1} - \frac{\partial F_1}{\partial x_2}$ in the plane, $\nabla \times F$ in space) measures rotation. Gradient fields have zero curl, and on $\mathbb{R}^n$ the converse holds; the rotation field $(-y, x)$ of the game $\min_x\max_y xy$ is not the gradient of anything.
    \item Multiple integrals are computed as iterated one-dimensional integrals (Fubini), which needs $\int |f| < \infty$ for unbounded functions or regions. Under a change of variables, small volumes scale by $|\det D\Phi|$; for example, $\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{x}}\, d\mathbf{x} = (2\pi)^{n/2}\sqrt{\det\Sigma}$. Line integrals of gradients depend only on the endpoints (integrated gradients). The divergence theorem $\int_V \Div F\, d\mathbf{x} = \int_{\partial V} F^\top\mathbf{n}\, dS$ (Green's theorem in the plane) gives integration by parts, $\int g\Div F = -\int \nabla g^\top F$, the basis of score matching.
    \item An ODE $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$ with a Lipschitz continuous $F$ has a unique solution for each initial value. Linear systems $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ are solved with eigenvectors, $\mathbf{x}(t) = \sum_i c_ie^{\lambda_i t}\mathbf{v}_i = e^{At}\mathbf{x}_0$, and the signs of the real parts of the eigenvalues decide stability. Along the gradient flow $\mathbf{x}' = -\nabla f(\mathbf{x})$, $f$ decreases; gradient descent is Euler's method for it, and its step size limit $\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$ is the stability limit of Euler's method for stiff problems.
    \item The flow $\varphi_t$ of a vector field $F$ expands volumes at the rate $\Div F$; for linear fields, $\det e^{At} = e^{t\tr A}$. A probability density moved by the flow satisfies the continuity equation $\frac{\partial p_t}{\partial t} = -\Div(p_tF)$, and along each solution, $\frac{d}{dt}\log p_t(\mathbf{x}(t)) = -\Div F(\mathbf{x}(t))$, which continuous normalizing flows use instead of a Jacobian determinant.
\end{itemize}

\section{Exercises}\label{sec:exercises}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
    \item\label{ex3:level-sets} \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Describe the level sets of $f(x, y) = x^2 + 4y^2$ and of $g(x, y) = xy$, and sketch a contour plot of each function.
        \item Let $f(x, y) = \frac{x^2 y}{x^4 + y^2}$ for $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$, and $f(0, 0) = 0$. Show that $f(t\mathbf{v}) \to 0$ as $t \to 0$ for every vector $\mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{R}^2$, i.e., along every line through the origin, but that $f$ is not continuous at the origin. (Hint: look at the parabola $y = x^2$.)
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that the derivative of the softplus function $s(x) = \log(1 + e^x)$ is the sigmoid function $\sigma$, and that $s$ is convex.
        \item Show that $\tanh(x) = 2\sigma(2x) - 1$, and use $\sigma' = \sigma(1 - \sigma)$ to show that $\tanh' = 1 - \tanh^2$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Compute the Taylor polynomial $T_3$ of $f(x) = \log(1 + x)$ at $0$, and use it to approximate $\log(1.1)$. Use Taylor's theorem to show that the error is at most $2.5 \cdot 10^{-5}$.
    \item Let $f(x, y, z) = x e^{yz} + y^2 z$. Compute $\nabla f$ and the directional derivative of $f$ at $(1, 0, 2)$ in the direction of the unit vector $\frac{1}{3}(1, 2, 2)^\top$. In which direction does $f$ decrease fastest at this point, and at which rate?
    \item Let $f(x, y) = \sqrt{|xy|}$. Show that both partial derivatives of $f$ at the origin exist, but that $f$ is not differentiable at the origin. (Hint: Proposition~\ref{prop:differentiable}.)
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Compute the Jacobian and the Jacobian determinant of $f(x, y) = (e^x\cos y, \, e^x\sin y)^\top$.
        \item Show that spherical coordinates $f(r, \theta, \varphi) = (r\sin\theta\cos\varphi, \, r\sin\theta\sin\varphi, \, r\cos\theta)^\top$ have the Jacobian determinant $r^2\sin\theta$. Use the change of variables formula (Remark~\ref{rem:change-of-variables}) to show that the ball of radius $R$ has the volume $\frac{4}{3}\pi R^3$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Let $\mathbf{x}(t)$ be a differentiable curve in $\mathbb{R}^n$. Show that $\frac{d}{dt}\norm{\mathbf{x}(t)}^2 = 2\mathbf{x}(t)^\top\mathbf{x}'(t)$, and conclude that if the curve lies on a sphere $\norm{\mathbf{x}} = r$, then its velocity $\mathbf{x}'(t)$ is always orthogonal to $\mathbf{x}(t)$.
        \item Let $f(u, v) = u^2 v$ and $g(x, y) = (x\cos y, \, x\sin y)^\top$. Compute $D(f \circ g)$ in two ways: as the product $Df(g(x, y))\,Dg(x, y)$, and by differentiating $f(g(x, y))$ directly.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item (Ridge regression.) Let $X \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times d}$, $\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^N$ and $\lambda > 0$, and let $f(\mathbf{w}) = \norm{X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}}^2 + \lambda\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2$. Compute $\nabla f$ and $\nabla^2 f$. Show that $X^\top X + \lambda I$ is invertible, and that the unique minimum of $f$ is $\mathbf{w}^* = (X^\top X + \lambda I)^{-1}X^\top\mathbf{y}$.
    \item Let $A$ be a symmetric $n \times n$ matrix and $R(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{x}}$ for $\mathbf{x} \neq \mathbf{0}$ (the Rayleigh quotient). Show that $\nabla R(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{2}{\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{x}}\big(A\mathbf{x} - R(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{x}\big)$, and conclude that the critical points of $R$ are exactly the eigenvectors of $A$.
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that $\nabla_X\tr(AXB) = A^\top B^\top$.
        \item Let $X \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times d}$ and $Y \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times k}$. Compute $\nabla_W\norm{XW - Y}_F^2$, and show that if $X$ has linearly independent columns, the minimum is $W^* = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top Y$. (This is linear regression with $k$ outputs.)
        \item Let $X$ be invertible. Show that $(X + H)^{-1} = X^{-1} - X^{-1}HX^{-1} + o(\norm{H}_F)$, by multiplying the right side by $X + H$. Conclude that $\nabla_X\tr(AX^{-1}) = -X^{-\top}A^\top X^{-\top}$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Let $\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^K$ be a probability vector (with $y_k \geq 0$ and $\sum_k y_k = 1$), and let
    \[\ell(\mathbf{z}) = -\sum_{k=1}^K y_k\log\softmax(\mathbf{z})_k\]
    be the cross-entropy with ``soft labels''. Show that $\nabla\ell(\mathbf{z}) = \softmax(\mathbf{z}) - \mathbf{y}$. Check that $\ell(\mathbf{z} + c\mathbf{1}) = \ell(\mathbf{z})$ for all $c \in \mathbb{R}$, and that $\nabla\ell(\mathbf{z})^\top\mathbf{1} = 0$. Why do these two facts fit together?
    \item Let $f(x_1, x_2) = x_1^2 x_2 + e^{x_1 x_2}$. Draw the computation graph of $f$, and compute $\nabla f(1, 0)$ with forward mode (two passes) and with reverse mode (one pass), as in Example~\ref{ex:autodiff}.
    \item Consider the network $\hat y = w_2\,\sigma(w_1 x + b_1) + b_2$ with one input, one hidden unit with the sigmoid activation function, and one output, and the loss $L = \frac{1}{2}(\hat y - y)^2$. Derive formulas for the four partial derivatives of $L$ with respect to $w_1$, $b_1$, $w_2$ and $b_2$, using backpropagation. Evaluate them for $x = 1$, $y = 0$, $w_1 = b_1 = b_2 = 0$ and $w_2 = 2$.
    \item Compute the gradient and the Hessian of $f(x, y) = \log(1 + x + 2y)$, and its second-order Taylor approximation at the origin. Use it to approximate $f(0.1, 0.05)$, and compare with the exact value. Is the Hessian at the origin positive definite, negative definite, or neither?
    \item Find and classify all critical points of $f(x, y) = x^4 + y^4 - 4xy$. Which of them are global minima?
    \item\label{ex3:inconclusive} Show that $f(x, y) = x^2 + y^4$ and $g(x, y) = x^2 - y^4$ have the same gradient and the same Hessian at the origin, but that the origin is a minimum of $f$ and a saddle point of $g$.
    \item Let $f(x_1, x_2) = x_1^2 + 9x_2^2$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item For which step sizes $\eta$ does gradient descent converge for every starting point?
        \item Find the best step size and the corresponding rate $\rho$. How many steps are needed to reduce the error by a factor $10^{-6}$?
        \item Substitute $x_2 = \frac{u}{3}$. How does the function look in the variables $(x_1, u)$, and how many steps does gradient descent need now with the best step size?
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that Newton's method finds the minimum of $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b}^\top\mathbf{x}$ (with $A$ symmetric positive definite) in one step, from every starting point.
        \item Let $B$ be an invertible matrix, $\mathbf{c}$ a vector, and $g(\mathbf{y}) = f(B\mathbf{y} + \mathbf{c})$. Show that if $\mathbf{x}_0 = B\mathbf{y}_0 + \mathbf{c}$, then the Newton iterates for $f$ and $g$ satisfy $\mathbf{x}_k = B\mathbf{y}_k + \mathbf{c}$ for all $k$. Show by an example that this is false for gradient descent.
        \item Apply Newton's method to $f(x) = x^4$, starting at $x_0 = 1$. Show that $x_k = (\frac{2}{3})^k$. Why is the convergence not quadratic here?
    \end{enumerate}
    \item\label{ex3:armijo} (Line search and Gauss--Newton.)
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Let $f$ be $L$-smooth, and let $0 < c < 1$ and $0 < \beta < 1$. Show that $f(\mathbf{x} - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x})) \leq f(\mathbf{x}) - c\eta\norm{\nabla f(\mathbf{x})}^2$ for all $\eta \leq \frac{2(1 - c)}{L}$, and that backtracking that multiplies $\eta$ by $\beta$ accepts a step size $\eta \geq \min\big(\bar\eta, \frac{2\beta(1 - c)}{L}\big)$.
        \item Show that for a linear model $\mathbf{r}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = A\boldsymbol{\theta} - \mathbf{y}$, where $A$ has linearly independent columns, the Gauss--Newton method reaches the least squares solution in one step, from every starting point.
        \item Show that the Levenberg--Marquardt step $\mathbf{h}_\lambda$ satisfies $\mathbf{h}_\lambda \to \mathbf{0}$ and $\lambda\mathbf{h}_\lambda \to -\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ as $\lambda \to \infty$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Which of the following functions are convex? Justify your answers. (a) $e^{x_1 + x_2}$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$; (b) $x_1 x_2$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$; (c) $\norm{\mathbf{x}}_1 + \norm{\mathbf{x}}^2$ on $\mathbb{R}^n$; (d) $\max(0, 1 - x)$ on $\mathbb{R}$; (e) $\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$; (f) $-\log(x_1 x_2)$ on $\{\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^2 : x_1 > 0, x_2 > 0\}$.
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that if $f$ is convex, then every sublevel set $\{\mathbf{x} : f(\mathbf{x}) \leq c\}$ is convex. Give an example of a function on $\mathbb{R}$ that is not convex, although all its sublevel sets are convex.
        \item Show that the gradient of a differentiable convex function satisfies $\big(\nabla f(\mathbf{x}) - \nabla f(\mathbf{y})\big)^\top(\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{y}) \geq 0$ for all $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}$. (Hint: add two inequalities of Theorem~\ref{thm:convex-first-order}.) What does this say for $n = 1$?
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Let $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ with $A = \diag(1, 4)$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Find the largest $\mu$ and the smallest $L$ for which $f$ is $\mu$-strongly convex and $L$-smooth.
        \item How many steps of gradient descent with $\eta = \frac{1}{L}$ does Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-rates} need to guarantee $f(\mathbf{x}_k) - f^* \leq 10^{-6}\big(f(\mathbf{x}_0) - f^*\big)$? Compare with the exact behavior of gradient descent on this function (Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-quadratic}).
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Compute the subdifferentials of $f(x) = \max(0, 1 - x)$ (the hinge loss) and of $g(x) = |x| + x^2$ at every point $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Use the condition $0 \in \partial f(x^*)$ to minimize $g$ and $h(x) = |x - 1| + |x - 2| + |x - 6|$. What is the minimum of $\sum_{i=1}^N |x - a_i|$ in general?
    \item (Weight decay.) Show that the proximal operator of $h(\mathbf{u}) = \frac{\lambda}{2}\norm{\mathbf{u}}^2$ is $\operatorname{prox}_{\eta h}(\mathbf{v}) = \frac{1}{1 + \eta\lambda}\mathbf{v}$. Write down the proximal gradient method for $f + h$, and compare it with gradient descent on $f + h$. Both shrink the weights towards $\mathbf{0}$ in every step, which is called weight decay.
    \item (Maximum entropy.) Use a Lagrange multiplier to find the probability vector $\mathbf{p} = (p_1, \ldots, p_n)$ with $p_i > 0$ and $\sum_i p_i = 1$ that maximizes the entropy $H(\mathbf{p}) = -\sum_i p_i\log p_i$. What is the maximal entropy?
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Use a Lagrange multiplier to find the point of the plane $x + 2y + 2z = 9$ that is closest to the origin. Compare with the formula for the distance to a hyperplane in Lecture~2.
        \item Find the rectangle with sides parallel to the axes and the largest area that fits into the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} \leq 1$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Solve the problem: minimize $x^2 + y^2$ subject to $x + y \geq 2$, using the KKT conditions. Which constraint is active, and what is the multiplier? What changes for the constraint $x + y \geq -2$?
    \item Find the dual function and solve the dual problem for (a) minimizing $x^2 + y^2$ subject to $x + y \geq 2$, and (b) minimizing $e^x$ subject to $x \geq 0$. Check that $d^* = p^*$ in both cases, and compare the dual solutions with the KKT multipliers.
    \item (Convex conjugates.)
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Compute the conjugates of $f(x) = ax^2 + bx$ (with $a > 0$), of $f(x) = |x|$, and of the softplus function $f(x) = \log(1 + e^x)$. (For the last one, the answer is a binary negative entropy.)
        \item Show that $f^*(\mathbf{y}) \geq -f(\mathbf{0})$, and that $(cf)^*(\mathbf{y}) = cf^*(\mathbf{y}/c)$ for $c > 0$.
        \item Write down the dual of the linear program: minimize $x_1 + 2x_2$ subject to $x_1 + x_2 \geq 1$, $x_1 \geq 0$ and $x_2 \geq 0$. Solve both problems, and check that their optimal values agree.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Compute the divergence and the curl of $F(x, y, z) = (yz, xz, xy)^\top$ and of $G(x, y, z) = (-y, x, z)^\top$. Find a function $f$ with $\nabla f = F$. Is $G$ a gradient field?
        \item Show that $\Div(gF) = g\Div F + \nabla g^\top F$ and $\Delta(fg) = g\Delta f + 2\nabla f^\top\nabla g + f\Delta g$.
        \item Show that $\Delta\log\norm{\mathbf{x}} = 0$ on $\mathbb{R}^2 \setminus \{\mathbf{0}\}$, and that $\Delta\norm{\mathbf{x}}^{2-n} = 0$ on $\mathbb{R}^n \setminus \{\mathbf{0}\}$ for $n \geq 3$. (Hint: $\nabla\norm{\mathbf{x}} = \frac{\mathbf{x}}{\norm{\mathbf{x}}}$, and use (b).)
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Let $F(\mathbf{x}) = A\mathbf{x}$ with $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that $\Div F = \tr A$, that $F$ is a gradient field if and only if $A$ is symmetric, and that then $F = \nabla(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x})$.
        \item For $A = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}$, split $A$ into its symmetric and skew-symmetric parts, and describe how the vector field stretches and rotates small neighborhoods of the origin. Compute $\Div F$ and $\curl F$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item\label{ex3:hutchinson} (Hutchinson's estimator.) Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be symmetric, and let $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \in \{-1, 1\}^n$ be a vector of signs. Show that $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top A\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} = \tr A + 2\sum_{i < j} A_{ij}\varepsilon_i\varepsilon_j$, and conclude that the average of $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top A\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}$ over all $2^n$ sign vectors is $\tr A$. For which matrices is every single estimate exact?
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Compute $\iint_D (x + y)\, dx\, dy$ for $D = \{(x, y) : 0 \leq x \leq 1, \ x^2 \leq y \leq x\}$, integrating first over $y$ and then over $x$, and in the other order.
        \item A random point $\mathbf{X}$ is uniformly distributed on the unit disk, i.e., it has the density $\frac{1}{\pi}$ on the disk and $0$ outside. Use polar coordinates to compute its average distance $\int \frac{1}{\pi}\norm{\mathbf{x}}\, d\mathbf{x}$ from the center. Find the density of the first coordinate $X_1$ by integrating out $x_2$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Differentiate $\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-ax^2}\, dx = \sqrt{\pi/a}$ (for $a > 0$) with respect to $a$ under the integral sign, and conclude that $\int_{-\infty}^\infty x^2e^{-x^2/2}\, dx = \sqrt{2\pi}$.
        \item (Stein's identity.) Let $\varphi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}$. Use integration by parts to show that for every polynomial $f$,
        \[\int_{-\infty}^\infty f'(x)\varphi(x)\, dx = \int_{-\infty}^\infty xf(x)\varphi(x)\, dx,\]
        and use it to compute $\int x^4\varphi(x)\, dx$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Verify the divergence theorem for $F(x, y) = (x^3, y^3)^\top$ on the unit disk.
        \item Use Green's theorem to show that the area enclosed by a closed curve $(x(t), y(t))$, $0 \leq t \leq T$, traversed counterclockwise, is $\frac{1}{2}\int_0^T \big(x(t)y'(t) - y(t)x'(t)\big)\, dt$. Compute the area of the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} \leq 1$ in this way.
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Solve the ODE $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ with $A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\mathbf{x}(0) = (1, 0)^\top$, using the eigenvectors of $A$. Which initial values give solutions that stay bounded as $t \to \infty$? Compute $e^{At}$.
    \item\label{ex3:euler-skew} \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that Euler's method for $x' = -\lambda x$ with $\lambda > 0$ gives $x_k = (1 - h\lambda)^k x_0$. For which step sizes $h$ does the numerical solution tend to $0$, like the exact solution? Relate the answer to Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-quadratic}.
        \item Let $A$ be skew-symmetric. Show that $Q(t) = e^{At}$ is orthogonal for all $t$, by differentiating $Q(t)^\top Q(t)$. Show that Euler's method for $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ increases $\norm{\mathbf{x}_k}$ in every step (unless $A\mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{0}$).
    \end{enumerate}
    \item\label{ex3:nilpotent} Let $A = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = -I + N$ with $N = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 2 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item Show that $A$ is not diagonalizable, and that $N^2 = 0$.
        \item Show that $e^{At} = e^{-t}(I + tN)$, by checking that it equals $I$ at $t = 0$ and that its derivative is $A$ times itself. Solve $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ with $\mathbf{x}(0) = (0, 1)^\top$.
        \item Check Liouville's formula $\det e^{At} = e^{t\tr A}$ for this matrix. By which factor does the flow change areas in the time $t$?
    \end{enumerate}
    \item Let $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$, and let $\mathbf{x}(0)$ be random with the density $p_0$. Use the change of variables formula (Remark~\ref{rem:change-of-variables}) and Proposition~\ref{prop:liouville} to show that $\log p_t(\mathbf{x}(t)) = \log p_0(\mathbf{x}(0)) - t\tr A$, and check that this agrees with Proposition~\ref{prop:continuity}. What is the distribution of $\mathbf{x}(t)$ if $\mathbf{x}(0) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, I)$?
\end{enumerate}

\section{Programming Exercises}\label{sec:programming}

% Inline code cannot be hyphenated, so allow a little extra stretch in this section.
\setlength{\emergencystretch}{3em}

As in the previous lectures, implement the algorithms yourself with NumPy, and use library routines only to check your results. Most exercises also need Matplotlib for plots. Start each file with
\begin{lstlisting}
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)  # random number generator for test data
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{P\arabic*.}, leftmargin=*]
    \item \textbf{Numerical differentiation and its errors.} Write functions \py{forward_diff(f, x, h)} and \py{central_diff(f, x, h)} for functions of one variable.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For $f = \sin$ at $x = 1$, compute the errors of both approximations of $f'(1) = \cos 1$ for $h = 10^{-1}, 10^{-2}, \ldots, 10^{-15}$, and plot them against $h$ with \py{plt.loglog}.
        \item Explain the V-shape of the curves: for large $h$, the error of Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-differences} dominates; for small $h$, rounding errors of size about $\frac{10^{-16}}{h}$ dominate. Which $h$ is best for each method, and how accurate is the result?
        \item Write a function \py{numerical_gradient(f, X, h=1e-5)} that computes the gradient of a scalar function \py{f} with respect to an array \py{X} of any shape by central differences, one entry at a time. (Hint: loop over \py{np.ndindex(X.shape)}.) You will use it in the following exercises.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Gradient checking.} For random vectors and matrices, compare every formula of Table~\ref{tab:gradients} with \py{numerical_gradient}, using the relative error $\frac{\norm{\mathbf{g} - \tilde{\mathbf{g}}}}{\norm{\mathbf{g}} + \norm{\tilde{\mathbf{g}}}}$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Compute $\softmax(\mathbf{z})$ for $\mathbf{z} = (1000, 0, -1000)^\top$ directly from the definition. What goes wrong? Since $\softmax(\mathbf{z} - c\mathbf{1}) = \softmax(\mathbf{z})$, you can subtract $c = \max_i z_i$ first. Implement a stable \py{softmax(z)} and a stable \py{logsumexp(z)} in this way, and check them against \py{scipy.special} if you have SciPy installed.
        \item Deliberately introduce a mistake into one of your gradient formulas (e.g., forget a transpose) and observe the relative error.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Contour plots and gradient fields.} Use \py{np.meshgrid}, \py{plt.contour} and \py{plt.quiver} to plot level curves and gradient vectors of $x^2 + y^2$, $x^2 - y^2$, $x^3 + y^3 - 3xy$ (mark the critical points of Example~\ref{ex:critical-points}) and the Rosenbrock function $(1 - x)^2 + 100(y - x^2)^2$ (use logarithmically spaced levels). Use \py{plt.gca().set_aspect("equal")} so that right angles look right.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Check Corollary~\ref{cor:level-sets} numerically: \py{cs = plt.contour(...)} returns an object, and \py{cs.get_paths()[i].vertices} contains the points of the $i$-th level curve. Compute the cosine of the angle between $\nabla f$ and the segments between consecutive points.
        \item Plot the graph of $x^2 - y^2$ as a surface. For this, create three-dimensional axes with the command \py{ax = plt.axes(projection="3d")}, and then use \py{ax.plot_surface}.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Automatic differentiation.} Implement reverse-mode automatic differentiation for scalar functions. Each \py{Var} stores its value, its parents with the local partial derivatives, and its adjoint \py{grad}:
\begin{lstlisting}
class Var:
    def __init__(self, value, parents=()):
        self.value = value
        self.parents = parents  # pairs (parent Var, local partial derivative)
        self.grad = 0.0

    def __add__(self, other):
        other = other if isinstance(other, Var) else Var(other)
        return Var(self.value + other.value, [(self, 1.0), (other, 1.0)])

    def __mul__(self, other):
        other = other if isinstance(other, Var) else Var(other)
        return Var(self.value * other.value,
                   [(self, other.value), (other, self.value)])

def log(x):
    return Var(np.log(x.value), [(x, 1.0 / x.value)])
\end{lstlisting}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Add subtraction, negation, division, powers with a constant exponent, \py{__radd__} and \py{__rmul__} (for expressions like \py{2 * x}), and the functions \py{exp}, \py{sin}, \py{cos} and \py{sigmoid}.
        \item Write a method \py{backward(self)}: list all variables that the output depends on in topological order (every variable after its parents, e.g., by a depth-first search), set the adjoint of the output to $1$, and go through the list in reverse order, adding \py{child.grad * local} to \py{parent.grad} for every pair \py{(parent, local)}.
        \item Check your implementation on Example~\ref{ex:autodiff} (you should get $5.5$ and $1.716$), on the logistic regression loss of Example~\ref{ex:logistic}, and on random examples against \py{numerical_gradient}.
        \item (Forward mode.) Implement a class \py{Dual} that stores a value and a derivative $\dot v$, with the forward rules of Section~\ref{sec:autodiff}, and check it on the same examples.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Logistic regression.} Generate two overlapping classes of points in the plane, and add a constant feature for the bias:
\begin{lstlisting}
N = 200
X0 = rng.normal([-1, -1], 1.0, size=(N // 2, 2))  # class 0
X1 = rng.normal([1, 1], 1.0, size=(N // 2, 2))    # class 1
X = np.hstack([np.vstack([X0, X1]), np.ones((N, 1))])
y = np.r_[np.zeros(N // 2), np.ones(N // 2)]
\end{lstlisting}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Implement the average cross-entropy loss, its gradient and its Hessian (Examples~\ref{ex:logistic} and~\ref{ex:ml-hessians}). For numerical stability, use $-y\log\sigma(z) - (1 - y)\log(1 - \sigma(z)) = \log(1 + e^z) - yz$ and \py{np.logaddexp(0, z)}. Check the gradient with \py{numerical_gradient}.
        \item Run gradient descent with $\eta = 0.1$, $1$ and $10$, and Newton's method, until $\norm{\nabla L(\mathbf{w})} < 10^{-6}$. Plot the loss against the number of iterations (with a logarithmic $y$-axis for $L(\mathbf{w}_k) - L(\mathbf{w}^*)$). How many iterations does each method need?
        \item Plot the data and the decision boundary $\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x} = 0$.
        \item Move the class centers to $(\pm 3, \pm 3)$, so that the classes are (very likely) linearly separable, and run gradient descent again. What happens to $\norm{\mathbf{w}_k}$ and to the loss, and why does the loss have no minimum? How does a ridge penalty $\lambda\norm{\mathbf{w}}^2$ change this?
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Training a neural network.} Fit $y = \sin x$ on $[-\pi, \pi]$ with a network with one hidden layer of $20$ units with the $\tanh$ activation function, as in Section~\ref{sec:autodiff}, with the loss $L = \frac{1}{2N}\sum_k (\hat y_k - y_k)^2$ over $N = 100$ equally spaced points.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Implement the forward and backward passes for a whole batch, with the inputs as the rows of a matrix (Example~\ref{ex:linear-layer}). Check all four gradients with \py{numerical_gradient} on a small network.
        \item Initialize $W_1$ with standard normal entries, $W_2$ with normal entries with standard deviation $\frac{1}{\sqrt{20}}$, and the biases with zeros. Train with gradient descent with $\eta = 0.05$ for $20\,000$ steps. Plot the loss (log scale) and the fitted function.
        \item Build a deep network with $10$ hidden layers of $20$ sigmoid units (initialize the weights between hidden layers with standard deviation $\frac{1}{\sqrt{20}}$), and print the norms $\norm{\nabla_{W_l} L}_F$ for all layers at initialization. What do you observe? Repeat with $\tanh$ and with ReLU.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Gradient descent on quadratic functions.} Let $f(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ with $A = \diag(1, \kappa)$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For $\kappa = 10$, plot the level curves of $f$ and the paths of gradient descent from $\mathbf{x}_0 = (8, 2)^\top$ for $\eta = 0.05$, $\frac{2}{11}$, $0.19$ and $0.21$, as in Figure~\ref{fig:gd}.
        \item For $\kappa = 1, 10, 100, 1000$ and $\eta = \frac{2}{1 + \kappa}$, count the steps needed to reach $\norm{\mathbf{x}_k} \leq 10^{-6}\norm{\mathbf{x}_0}$ from $\mathbf{x}_0 = (1, 1)^\top$, and compare with Theorem~\ref{thm:gd-quadratic}.
        \item Implement the heavy ball method with $\eta = \frac{4}{(1 + \sqrt{\kappa})^2}$ and $\beta = \big(\frac{\sqrt{\kappa} - 1}{\sqrt{\kappa} + 1}\big)^2$, and repeat the count. How does the number of steps grow with $\kappa$ now?
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{The Rosenbrock function.} Let $f(x, y) = (1 - x)^2 + 100(y - x^2)^2$, whose minimum is $(1, 1)$, at the bottom of a long, curved valley.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Compute $\nabla f$ and $\nabla^2 f$ by hand, and check them with \py{numerical_gradient}. Check that $(1, 1)$ is the only critical point, and that the Hessian there is positive definite. What is its condition number?
        \item Starting at $(-1.2, 1)$, run gradient descent with $\eta = 10^{-3}$ until $\norm{\mathbf{x}_k - (1, 1)^\top} < 10^{-4}$. How many steps does it need? What happens for $\eta = 3 \cdot 10^{-3}$? (Look at consecutive iterates, and compare $\eta$ with $\frac{2}{\lambda_{\max}}$ for the Hessian at $(1, 1)$.)
        \item Implement gradient descent with a backtracking line search: in each step, start with $\eta = 1$ and halve it until $f(\mathbf{x} - \eta\nabla f(\mathbf{x})) \leq f(\mathbf{x}) - 10^{-4}\eta\norm{\nabla f(\mathbf{x})}^2$.
        \item Run Newton's method from the same starting point. Plot the paths of all three methods on a contour plot, and compare the numbers of steps.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Stochastic gradient descent.} Generate a linear regression problem with many data points:
\begin{lstlisting}
N, d = 10_000, 20
X = rng.normal(size=(N, d))
w_true = rng.normal(size=d)
y = X @ w_true + 0.5 * rng.normal(size=N)
\end{lstlisting}
    and let $L(\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{N}\norm{X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}}^2$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Compute the minimum $\mathbf{w}^*$ with \py{np.linalg.lstsq}, and the eigenvalues of the Hessian $\frac{2}{N}X^\top X$. Which step sizes are safe for gradient descent?
        \item Compare gradient descent ($\eta = 0.1$) with SGD with mini-batches of size $1$, $32$ and $256$ (draw the indices with \py{rng.integers(0, N, size=B)}), with step sizes $0.01$, $0.05$ and $0.1$. Plot $L(\mathbf{w}_k) - L(\mathbf{w}^*)$ against the number of processed data points (one gradient step of full gradient descent processes $N$ points).
        \item With a constant step size, SGD stops improving at some level. Try the decreasing step sizes $\eta_k = \frac{\eta_0}{1 + k/1000}$ and compare.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Constrained optimization.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item (Projected gradient ascent.) For a random symmetric positive semidefinite matrix $A = B^\top B$, maximize $\mathbf{x}^\top A\mathbf{x}$ on the unit sphere by repeating $\mathbf{x} \leftarrow \frac{\mathbf{x} + \eta A\mathbf{x}}{\norm{\mathbf{x} + \eta A\mathbf{x}}}$. Compare the result with the top eigenvector from \py{np.linalg.eigh} (Example~\ref{ex:lagrange-eigen}). What happens as $\eta \to \infty$? (Compare with power iteration from Lecture~1.)
        \item (Projected gradient descent.) Let \py{A = rng.normal(size=(30, 10))}, let \py{x_true} be a random vector of norm $3$, and \py{b = A @ x_true + 0.1 * rng.normal(size=30)}. Minimize $\norm{A\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{b}}^2$ subject to $\norm{\mathbf{x}} \leq 1$, by alternating gradient steps with step size $\frac{1}{L}$, where $L = 2\norm{A}_2^2$, with the projection of Example~\ref{ex:kkt-ball}. At the result, compute the multiplier $\mu$ from the stationarity condition, and check all four KKT conditions.
        \item (Minimum norm solutions.) For a random $3 \times 6$ matrix $A$ and $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^3$, compute $\mathbf{x}^* = A^\top(AA^\top)^{-1}\mathbf{b}$ (Example~\ref{ex:min-norm}) and compare it with \py{np.linalg.pinv(A) @ b}. Get a basis of $\Null(A)$ from the SVD (the last rows of $V^\top$), and check that adding a null space vector to $\mathbf{x}^*$ gives another solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ with a larger norm.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Vector fields.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Plot the three vector fields of Figure~\ref{fig:vector-fields} and the vortex field of Example~\ref{ex:vortex} with \py{plt.quiver}. Compute their divergence and curl on a grid with \py{np.gradient}, which approximates partial derivatives by central differences, and compare with the exact values.
        \item (Hutchinson's estimator.) Let $F(\mathbf{x}) = \tanh(W\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{b})$ (entrywise) with a random $100 \times 100$ matrix $W$ and a random vector $\mathbf{b}$. Show that $\Div F(\mathbf{x}) = \sum_i (1 - \tanh^2 z_i)W_{ii}$ with $\mathbf{z} = W\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{b}$, and that $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top DF(\mathbf{x}) = \big(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon} \odot (1 - \tanh^2\mathbf{z})\big)^\top W$. At a random point, average $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}^\top DF(\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}$ over $m$ random sign vectors, and plot the error against $m = 1, 10, 100, 1000$ on a log-log scale. What is the slope?
        \item (Game dynamics.) For $f(x, y) = xy$, start at $(1, 1)$ and run $1000$ steps with $\eta = 0.1$ of (i) simultaneous gradient descent--ascent, $(x, y) \leftarrow (x - \eta y, \, y + \eta x)$; (ii) alternating updates, $x \leftarrow x - \eta y$ and then $y \leftarrow y + \eta x$ with the new $x$; (iii) the extragradient method, which computes the trial point $(\tilde x, \tilde y) = (x - \eta y, \, y + \eta x)$ and then updates $(x, y) \leftarrow (x - \eta\tilde y, \, y + \eta\tilde x)$ with the gradients at the trial point. Plot the paths and $\norm{(x_k, y_k)}$ against $k$. Show that (i) multiplies the norm by $\sqrt{1 + \eta^2}$ in each step, and (iii) by $\sqrt{1 - \eta^2 + \eta^4}$.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Multiple integrals.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item By Fubini's theorem, $\int_{[0, 1]^d} e^{-\norm{\mathbf{x}}^2}\, d\mathbf{x} = \big(\int_0^1 e^{-t^2}\, dt\big)^d$. Compute the one-dimensional integral accurately with the midpoint rule (the Riemann sum with the midpoints of the small intervals). Then approximate the $d$-dimensional integral for $d = 1, 2, 3, 5, 8$ by the midpoint rule on a grid with $k$ points per axis ($k^d$ evaluations of the function), and by the average of the function over $N$ uniformly random points (Monte Carlo, Lecture~4) with the same number of evaluations. Plot the errors against the number of evaluations. Which method is better in low dimensions, and which in high dimensions?
        \item Check the divergence theorem numerically for $F(x, y) = (x^3 + y, \, \sin x + y^2)^\top$ on the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 \leq 1$: compute the left side on a fine grid, using the indicator function of the ellipse, and the right side with the parametrization $\gamma(t) = (2\cos t, \sin t)$, for which $\mathbf{n}\norm{\gamma'(t)} = (\gamma_2'(t), -\gamma_1'(t))^\top$.
        \item (Integrated gradients.) For the logistic regression model $f(\mathbf{x}) = \sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{x})$ of P5, compute the attributions of Example~\ref{ex:integrated-gradients} for a few data points with the baseline $\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{0}$ (approximate the integrals by the midpoint rule with $50$ points), and check that they add up to $f(\mathbf{x}) - f(\mathbf{x}')$. Compare with the attributions $x_i\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}(\mathbf{x})$ (``gradient times input'').
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{ODEs and gradient flow.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Implement Euler's method for $\mathbf{x}' = F(\mathbf{x})$. For $A = \begin{pmatrix} -2 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 \end{pmatrix}$ and $\mathbf{x}_0 = (2, 0)^\top$, compare the numerical solution at $t = 1$ with the exact solution of Example~\ref{ex:linear-ode}, for $h = 0.1, 0.05, 0.025, \ldots$. Plot the error against $h$ on a log-log scale; what is the slope? If you have SciPy installed, also check your solver against \py{scipy.integrate.solve_ivp}.
        \item Implement the fourth-order Runge--Kutta method, $\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \frac{h}{6}(\mathbf{k}_1 + 2\mathbf{k}_2 + 2\mathbf{k}_3 + \mathbf{k}_4)$ with $\mathbf{k}_1 = F(\mathbf{x}_k)$, $\mathbf{k}_2 = F(\mathbf{x}_k + \frac{h}{2}\mathbf{k}_1)$, $\mathbf{k}_3 = F(\mathbf{x}_k + \frac{h}{2}\mathbf{k}_2)$, $\mathbf{k}_4 = F(\mathbf{x}_k + h\mathbf{k}_3)$, and repeat the error plot.
        \item Plot the vector field of Figure~\ref{fig:ode} with \py{plt.quiver}, together with numerical solutions. Do the same for the gradient flow of the Rosenbrock function, and compare the solution of the flow (computed with a small step) with the gradient descent iterates of P8 for $\eta = 10^{-3}$ and $\eta = 3 \cdot 10^{-3}$.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Flows and volumes.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Draw $10\,000$ samples from $\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, I)$ in $\mathbb{R}^2$, and move them with the flow of $\mathbf{x}' = A\mathbf{x}$ for the matrix $A$ of Figure~\ref{fig:ode} up to $t = 1$, using your Runge--Kutta method from P13. Compare the sample covariance matrix with $e^{At}e^{A^\top t}$, and its determinant with $e^{2t\tr A}$.
        \item The pendulum $x_1' = x_2$, $x_2' = -\sin x_1$ has $\Div F = 0$, and with friction, $x_2' = -\sin x_1 - \gamma x_2$, it has $\Div F = -\gamma$. Represent a small disk around $(1, 0)$ by $200$ points on its boundary circle, move them with the flow, and compute the enclosed area over time with the shoelace formula $\frac{1}{2}\sum_i (x_iy_{i+1} - x_{i+1}y_i)$. Compare with the prediction of the divergence for $\gamma = 0$ and $\gamma = 0.5$, and plot the deformed disks.
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{The lasso.} Generate a regression problem in which only $5$ of $50$ features matter:
\begin{lstlisting}
N, d = 100, 50
X = rng.normal(size=(N, d))
w_true = np.zeros(d)
w_true[:5] = [3, -2, 1.5, -1, 0.5]
y = X @ w_true + 0.3 * rng.normal(size=N)
\end{lstlisting}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Implement soft thresholding (Proposition~\ref{prop:soft-threshold}) and the iterative soft-thresholding algorithm for $F(\mathbf{w}) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}}^2 + \lambda\norm{\mathbf{w}}_1$, with the step size $\eta = 1/\norm{X}_2^2$ (the smoothness constant of the first term). Check that $F(\mathbf{w}_k)$ decreases in every step (Theorem~\ref{thm:prox-gradient}).
        \item For $\lambda = 1, 10, 50$, count the nonzero weights of the solution, and compare them with $\mathbf{w}_{\text{true}}$ and with the least squares solution. Check the optimality condition of Theorem~\ref{thm:composite-optimality}: $|(X^\top(X\mathbf{w} - \mathbf{y}))_i| \leq \lambda$ for the zero weights, and $= \lambda$ for the others.
        \item Plot the weights of the solution as functions of $\lambda$ (the lasso path). At which values of $\lambda$ do the true features enter the model?
    \end{itemize}

    \item \textbf{Line search, Gauss--Newton and Hessian-vector products.}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item Implement gradient descent with backtracking line search (Proposition~\ref{prop:backtracking}, with $\bar\eta = 1$), and apply it to the Rosenbrock function $f(x, y) = (1 - x)^2 + 100(y - x^2)^2$ from $(-1.2, 1)$. Compare with the constant step size $\eta = 10^{-3}$: how many iterations and how many evaluations of $f$ does each method need to reach $\norm{\nabla f} < 10^{-4}$?
        \item Generate $20$ data points $y_i = 2e^{-0.5t_i} + 0.05\varepsilon_i$ with $t_i$ equally spaced in $[0, 10]$ and standard normal $\varepsilon_i$, and fit the model $y = ae^{-bt}$ with Gauss--Newton and with Levenberg--Marquardt, starting from $(a, b) = (1, 2)$. Plot $f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_k)$ for both methods.
        \item For the Rosenbrock function, compute Hessian-vector products as $\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\big(\nabla f(\mathbf{x} + \varepsilon\mathbf{v}) - \nabla f(\mathbf{x} - \varepsilon\mathbf{v})\big)$, compare them with the exact Hessian, and use them in power iteration to find the largest eigenvalue of the Hessian at the minimum $(1, 1)$. What does it say about the largest stable step size of gradient descent near the minimum?
    \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}
